Dear Secretary Reese: 
Pursuant to section 205 of the Federal Power Act (“FPA”)1 and Part 35 of the 
Commission’s regulations,2 the PJM Transmission Owners,3 acting through the Consolidated 
Transmission Owners Agreement,4 submit for filing revisions to the PJM Interconnection, L.L.C. 
(“PJM”) Open Access Transmission Tariff (“Tariff”).5   
As discussed below, and supported by the Direct Joint Testimony of Peter Blazunas and 
Hanhan Hammer, the proposed Tariff revisions establish rates, cost allocation, cost recovery 
mechanisms, financial protections, revenue-crediting requirements, and transparency measures 
associated with transmission service to be taken by Eligible Customers6 on behalf of Co-Located 
Loads and Large Loads.7  These revisions are both responsive to and consistent with the 
preliminary findings made by the Commission in its June 18, 2026 Order Instituting Proceeding

Under Section 206 of the Federal Power Act in Docket No. EL26-67-000 (“Show Cause Order”),8 
and also comply with the Commission’s June 18, 2026 Order on Rehearing, Clarification, 
Compliance Filing and Paper Hearing in Docket Nos. EL25-49, et al. (“Co-Located Load Paper 
Hearing Order”).9  Additionally, the proposed tariff revisions have been designed to be consistent 
with PJM’s responsive section 205 filings establishing provisions for: (1) a new Fast Energization 
Process for generation interconnection and Large Load-related transmission service requests; (2) 
co-location and behind-the-meter generation; and (3) ride-through.   
 
This filing consists of several key elements.  With respect to each of these components, the 
PJM Transmission Owners have carefully considered and accounted for the need for ratepayer 
protections, Large Load and generation customer cost certainty and commercial stability, and cost 
transparency.  The PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal achieves a just and reasonable balance by 
protecting everyday customers from unfairly bearing the significant transmission-related costs 
necessary to connect and serve new Large Load and generation customers.  At the same time, it 
promotes speed-to-power, which is critical to supporting the accelerating pace of Artificial 
Intelligence, the growth of domestic manufacturing, and other information technology 
developments.  The elements are as follows: 
 
1. A pro forma Cost Recovery Agreement and related implementing Tariff provisions.  
The pro forma Cost Recovery Agreement (“CRA”) and related Tariff provisions are 
designed to address the Commission’s concerns that the Tariff lacks an adequate 
mechanism to mitigate the risk of cost shifting among transmission customers.  
Consistent with the Show Cause Order, the pro forma CRA will require Eligible 
Customers taking service on behalf of Large Load customers to make a minimum 
contribution towards transmission owners’ revenue requirements, along with a 
shortfall payment and crediting mechanism and financial security obligations, to 
ensure that each customer’s minimum commitment is satisfied over the term of the 
CRA.  The PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal also appropriately recognizes, 
consistent with the Commission’s acknowledgement,10 that voluntary cost recovery 
agreements and other ratepayer protection mechanisms have been, and continue to be, 
utilized at the retail and wholesale level by utilities within PJM.  Therefore, the PJM 
Transmission Owners’ proposal makes clear that PJM Transmission Owners may 
continue to utilize existing and  new consumer protection arrangements, as alternatives 
to the pro forma CRA, so long as those arrangements result in comparable protections 
and are accepted for filing by the Commission.

2. Rates for Transmission Services Available to Large Loads and Co-Located Loads.  
The PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal contains rates for the new services the 
Commission has required to be made available to Eligible Customers taking service 
on behalf of Co-Located Loads and that the Commission preliminarily found should 
also be extended to certain Large Loads.  These include Firm Contract Demand 
Transmission Service (“FCDS”), Non-Firm Contract Demand Transmission Service 
(“NFCDS”), and Interim Network Integration Transmission Service (“Interim NITS”).  
The rates for these services are largely identical to those proposed by the PJM 
Transmission Owners and approved by the Commission in the Co-Located Load Paper 
Hearing Order, with the modifications required by the Commission in that Order.  The 
PJM Transmission Owners have also included, consistent with the Co-Located Load 
Paper Hearing Order, financial penalties for unreserved use by Eligible Customers 
taking one of the new transmission services on behalf of Co-Located Loads or Large 
Loads.      
 
3. Cost Allocation and Recovery of Upgrades Necessary to Provide Interconnection and 
Transmission Service.  The PJM Transmission Owners are proposing a methodology 
for allocating and recovering the costs of transmission upgrades, referred to as 
Interconnection Reliability Reinforcements (“IRRs”), resulting from PJM’s proposed 
Fast Energization Process.11  The allocation methodology is based on a modified 
version of PJM’s existing proportional impact allocation methodology for customers 
in its New Service Request process.  Cost responsibility will be assigned to those 
Zones containing Large Loads being studied for transmission service in the PJM Fast 
Energization process. Those costs will be recovered through a combination of firm 
transmission service rates to customers in the Zones where the IRRs are located, along 
with a new Interconnection Reliability Reinforcement Charge assessed to Zones based 
on the calculated impact of the Large Loads within the Zones.  As it pertains to the 
proportional impact-driven share of costs attributable to Co-Located Loads and Large 
Loads, this allocation methodology will apply to each portfolio of IRRs identified 
through PJM’s Fast Energization Process for 15 years.12  After that 15-year period, the 
remaining costs of the IRRs will be subject to regional allocation using the then-current 
Schedule 12 methodology that applies to Regional Transmission Expansion Plan 
(“RTEP”) reliability projects.  The proportional impact-driven share of costs 
attributable to generators in the Fast Energization Process will be recovered through a 
fee billed and collected by PJM over a 15-year period and credited back to customers 
in the Zones that are allocated IRR costs. 
 
 
11  
PJM’s proposed Fast Energization Process is an annual process that evaluates both generator interconnection 
and Large Load transmission service requests through a single cluster study, resulting in a consolidated portfolio of 
IRRs needed to provide interconnection and transmission service to the cluster of generator and Large Load-related 
service requests. 
12  
References throughout this filing to the “portfolio” are those projects (e.g., upgrades, transmission 
reinforcements) identified by PJM that will be required to accommodate both generators seeking interconnection and 
Eligible Customers seeking transmission service on behalf of Large Loads in each study cycle.

4. Transparency and Customer Protection Commitments.  In addition to the basic cost 
allocation and rate design features of the PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal, the 
PJM Transmission Owners are committing to make, on compliance, necessary changes 
to their formula rate templates to: (1) provide transparency to state regulators and other 
interested parties regarding the costs of transmission facilities being constructed by 
each Transmission Owner to provide transmission service to Large Loads; and (2) 
adopt a mechanism designed to protect wholesale transmission customers not 
regulated at the retail level (municipals, cooperatives, and other applicable load-
serving entities) from having to bear excessive large-load-related transmission upgrade 
costs.     
 
Each of these elements is designed to align with the Fast Energization proposal being 
submitted by PJM on the same date as this filing.  Based on information provided to stakeholders,13 
PJM is proposing an approach that would process and study requests for both generator 
interconnection and transmission service on behalf of Large Loads in a single annual cluster, 
resulting in a portfolio of IRRs necessary to accommodate the aggregate of interconnection and 
transmission service requests in each cluster cycle.  Although each of the elements of the 
Transmission Owners’ proposal is designed to work in tandem with PJM’s proposed Fast 
Energization Process, the essential elements are independently just and reasonable, and 
importantly, satisfy the Commission’s preliminary findings in the Show Cause Order.  In other 
words, regardless of how the Commission acts on PJM’s filing, the Commission can and should 
determine that the key aspects of the PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal, as discussed below, 
are just and reasonable and should be accepted.  This remains the case even if certain incremental 
changes are required on compliance to ensure compatibility with the determinations the 
Commission makes regarding the planning and study process for generator interconnection and 
transmission service customers.

I. 
EXECUTIVE SUMMARY 
The proposed Tariff revisions contained in this filing establish rates, cost allocation, cost 
recovery mechanisms, financial protections, revenue-crediting requirements, and transparency 
measures associated with transmission service to be taken on behalf of Co-Located Loads and 
Large Loads.  These revisions are responsive to and consistent with both the preliminary findings 
made by the Commission in its June 18, 2026 Show Cause Order as well as the Commission’s Co-
Located Load Paper Hearing Order issued on the same date.  The proposed Tariff revisions are 
designed to work in tandem with PJM’s section 205 filing submitted on the same date, which 
proposes a Fast Energization Process that combines both generator interconnection and 
transmission service requests made on behalf of Co-Located Loads and Large Loads into a single 
annual cluster process.  The process would result in a consolidated portfolio of IRRs needed to 
provide interconnection and transmission service to the cluster of generator and Large Load-
related service requests.  However, the key elements of the PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal 
are independently just and reasonable and fully address both the Commission’s preliminary 
findings in the Show Cause Order and the policies underlying those findings.   
 
The Commission has explained that the integration of significant new Large Loads is an 
urgent challenge requiring a timely, orderly, and nondiscriminatory path to transmission service 
that preserves reliability and affordability.14  The PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal reflects the 
need for prompt action and meets the Commission’s objective to provide ratepayer protections, as 
identified in the Show Cause Order.  The PJM Transmission Owners’ proposed revisions therefore 
strike a practical balance.  Namely, they provide sufficient terms to ensure that Large Loads and 
other responsible entities bear their share of the costs and revenue risks associated with their 
requests, thereby protecting other customers, without imposing unnecessary complexity or 
unpredictability that could jeopardize speed to power by impeding efficient planning. 
Consistent with the Commission’s findings that it was not seeking to alter or call into 
question existing cost allocation methods for transmission upgrades,15 existing zonal NITS and 
formula rate structures remain the starting point for recovering applicable transmission revenue 
requirements, including transmission upgrades necessary to facilitate transmission service 
requested by Eligible Customers on behalf of Large Loads.  The PJM Transmission Owners’ 
proposed Tariff revisions supplement those structures with measures addressing distinct risks 
associated with Large Load integration, including secured commitments associated with the 
requested transmission service, potential shortfalls in or loss of anticipated firm service revenues, 
unreserved transmission use, and potential double recovery.  For instance, and as explained further 
herein, the PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal includes: 
1. An obligation on Large Loads to enter into a pro forma CRA, or comparable alternative 
agreement, to ensure the Large Load Customer’s committed contribution toward the 
applicable Transmission Owner’s transmission revenue requirement and to require the 
Large Load Customer to provide Credit Support securing the associated payment 
 
14  
Show Cause Order at PP 1-7, 35-39. 
15  
Id. at P 81.

PDF page 6

Honorable Debbie-Anne A. Reese 
November 16, 2026 
Page 6 
 
 
 
 
obligations.  The pro forma CRA includes applicable shortfall and termination 
payments to reduce the revenue requirement that would otherwise need to be recovered 
from other transmission customers if the Large Load fails to take the committed level 
of transmission service, along with requirements to provide and maintain robust 
financial security.  The PJM Transmission Owners propose to require Large Loads to 
enter into CRAs, or comparable agreements prior to entering PJM’s Fast Energization 
Process to ensure a significant financial commitment on the part of the Large Load 
Customer and thereby deter speculative or duplicative transmission service requests.  
Any amounts collected from the Large Load Customer as a result of terminating the 
CRA prior to the commencement of service will first go towards defraying any costs 
associated with transmission development that is no longer needed, with any remaining 
amounts credited against the Transmission Owner’s revenue requirement.  Any 
termination amount collected from the Large Load Customer after service commences 
will be entirely credited against the Transmission Owner’s revenue requirement, solely 
to the benefit of other customers.  These mechanisms mitigate the risks of potential cost 
shifts to other transmission customers.    
2. A methodology to allocate the costs of the portfolio of transmission upgrades, known 
as Interconnection Reliability Reinforcements, identified by PJM in its Fast 
Energization Process as needed to accommodate the generator interconnection and 
transmission service requests in each annual cycle.  This methodology will allocate cost 
responsibility among PJM Zones – and only those zones with Large Loads studied 
within an annual cycle – in a manner roughly commensurate with benefits by utilizing 
modeled electrical impacts on identified constraints.  This approach is consistent with 
the proportional impact methodology approved by the Commission and currently used 
by PJM to allocate costs associated with its current generator interconnection process.  
This methodology will also determine the proportional impact associated with 
generators seeking interconnection through the Fast Energization Process annual cycle, 
which will be recovered through a fee assessed over a 15-year period.  The PJM 
Transmission Owners are also proposing that all IRRs be allocated to Zones containing 
Large Load-related transmission requests for 15 years, after which point the remaining 
costs will be allocated using the then-current general regional cost allocation 
methodology.  This approach balances a recognition of the initial Large Load-related 
drivers of IRRs, with the fact that changes in system conditions, use and topology will 
result in a broader set of beneficiaries over time. 
 
3. A commitment to include in their transmission owner-specific formula rates, on 
compliance, additional measures to protect municipal utilities, cooperatives, and other 
direct wholesale customers from bearing an excessive share of Large Load-related 
zonal costs.  The PJM Transmission Owners are also committing to include in their 
formula rates cost information regarding transmission upgrades needed to provide 
service to Large Loads.  This information will address both the transparency concerns 
identified by the Commission in the Show Cause Order, as well as provide state 
authorities with information relevant to any further allocation of those costs at the retail 
level.

PDF page 7

Honorable Debbie-Anne A. Reese 
November 16, 2026 
Page 7 
 
 
 
 
 
4. Extending the rate design elements associated with the new transmission services 
required by the Commission in the Co-Located Load Paper Hearing Order to 
encompass the ability of Large Loads to take these new transmission services, which 
the PJM Transmission Owners understand PJM will be proposing in its own section 
205 filing.  The rate design also includes appropriate crediting provisions for the new 
transmission services required by the Commission to ensure that non-firm transmission 
revenues are used to offset rates paid by firm transmission customers.  Moreover, the 
PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal includes financial penalties for unreserved use of 
the transmission system that will apply to transmission service being taken on behalf 
of both Co-Located Load and Large Loads.  These penalties will work in coordination 
with PJM’s operational consequences both to ensure the reliable operation of the 
system and protect other transmission ratepayers from unfairly subsidizing customers 
that fail to abide by their contractual limits.   
 
II. 
BACKGROUND 
A. 
PJM Transmission Owner Filing Rights 
As the Commission has noted, the PJM Transmission Owners have exclusive and unilateral 
rights regarding the cost allocation provisions of Schedule 12 of the Tariff.16  The PJM 
Transmission Owners relied on those same authorities in their February 23, 2026 paper hearing 
brief in explaining their rights and obligations under the Consolidated Transmission Owners 
Agreement to develop and file the rates for the new transmission services that the Commission has 
required for Co-Located and Large Loads.17  The Show Cause Order recognized the PJM 
Transmission Owners may submit this filing proposing revisions to the Tariff pursuant to their 
applicable FPA section 205 filing rights.18  
B. 
Procedural Background 
1. 
Co-Located Load Orders and Remaining Compliance Obligations. 
On February 20, 2025, the Commission instituted a proceeding under section 206 of the 
FPA based on its preliminary concern that the Tariff did not address with sufficient clarity or 
consistency the rates, terms, and conditions applicable to co-location arrangements.19

On December 18, 2025, the Commission found the Tariff to be unjust and unreasonable 
and established a framework under which an Eligible Customer taking transmission service on 
behalf of Co-Located Load must take: (1) NITS, including interim non-firm service pending 
completion of the Network Upgrades required for permanent NITS; (2) FCDS; or (3) NFCDS.  
The Commission also established a paper hearing to determine the just and reasonable rates, terms, 
and conditions for those services.20 
The PJM Transmission Owners submitted an initial brief on February 23, 2026, a response 
to PJM’s initial brief on March 25, 2026, and a reply brief on April 24, 2026, addressing the rates 
and related measures applicable to the new services.21  On June 18, 2026, the Commission resolved 
the paper hearing issues and directed further compliance filings by PJM and the PJM Transmission 
Owners.  Among other matters, the Commission addressed the rate treatment for FCDS, NFCDS, 
and Interim NITS and the imposition of financial penalties for unreserved transmission use.22  
Notably, the Commission accepted the PJM Transmission Owners’ proposals with respect to the 
rates for FCDS and Interim NITS, as well as financial penalties for unreserved use.  The 
Commission, however, required the PJM Transmission Owners to adopt a lower minimum rate or 
discount for NFCDS below the Firm Contract Demand rate.   
On August 17, 2026, PJM and the PJM Transmission Owners submitted partial compliance 
filings implementing Interim NITS.23  The PJM Transmission Owners’ filing established the 
Interim NITS rate and made related conforming Tariff revisions.24  The present filing implements 
the PJM Transmission Owners’ remaining compliance obligations associated with the Co-Located 
Load Paper Hearing Order and makes the revisions to the August 17, 2026 filing required to 
integrate the remaining new services and their application to eligible Large Loads.25

2. 
Large Load Show Cause Order and Section 205 Proposal. 
On June 18, 2026, the Commission instituted a separate section 206 proceeding concerning 
the integration of Large Loads in PJM. The Commission preliminarily identified concerns 
regarding: 
1. The application, study, and continuing operational requirements applicable to Eligible 
Customers seeking transmission service on behalf of Large Loads; 
2. Transparency concerning the Network Upgrade costs associated with that service, a 
pro forma CRA to mitigate cost shifting risks, and a mechanism to ensure CRA 
payments are credited toward the revenue requirement consistent with the 
Commission’s cost-of-service regulations; 
3. Transmission-service options for flexible Large Loads; and 
4. The rates, terms, and conditions applicable to generation serving electrically proximate 
Large Load or Co-Located Load.26 
3. 
Abeyance Request. 
The Commission expressly contemplated that PJM and the PJM Transmission Owners 
could address some or all of the concerns identified in the Show Cause Order through affirmative 
section 205 filings covering matters within their respective filing rights.27  On August 14, 2026, 
the Commission granted the PJM Transmission Owners’ requested abeyance to permit the PJM 
Transmission Owners to develop one or more section 205 filings.28  The Commission explained 
that the anticipated PJM Transmission Owner filings would address matters including rates for the 
new transmission services, pro forma CRAs, and financial penalties.  It also provided that a 
respondent’s submission of a section 205 filing by November 16, 2026, would suspend that 
respondent’s obligation to answer the Show Cause Order pending further Commission action.29  
The PJM Transmission Owners submit the present filing following that section 205 path.  
The proposed Tariff revisions address the Commission’s preliminary findings, and the PJM 
Transmission Owners therefore request that the Commission accept the revisions under section 
205 and, as appropriate, resolve, terminate, or continue to hold in abeyance the corresponding 
aspects of the pending section 206 proceeding.  Specifically, the pro forma CRA, the application 
of the new transmission-service rates and financial penalties to eligible Large Loads, and the 
proposed transparency and customer protection measures address the Show Cause Order’s 
preliminary findings within the PJM Transmission Owners’ applicable rate design and cost 
allocation filing rights.  The filing also establishes the cost allocation and cost recovery treatment

III. 
DISCUSSION 
A. 
Pro Forma Cost Recovery Agreement and Related Tariff Provisions 
 
The PJM Transmission Owners’ proposed pro forma CRA addresses the Show Cause 
Order’s preliminary findings that the PJM tariff should include an agreement requiring a minimum 
contribution towards a transmission owner’s revenue requirement to protect against cost shifting 
if a Large Load does not go into service or ends up taking less transmission service than 
anticipated.30  As further detailed in the Blazunas and Hammer Direct Joint Testimony and below, 
the pro forma CRA accomplishes this through imposing on Large Load Customers a financially 
secured minimum transmission-revenue contribution commitment associated with the amount of 
firm transmission service requested from PJM on behalf of a Large Load.31  The CRA is executed 
after completion of the applicable Transmission Owner’s retail interconnection study process and 
before the Eligible Customer proceeds through PJM’s applicable transmission-service process. 
The Large Load Customer must identify and reflect in the CRA its: (i) anticipated load demand, 
(ii) proposed schedule for ramping up such demand, if any, and (iii) anticipated dates for each 
stage of service.  Together with the corresponding amount and type of firm transmission service 
that an Eligible Customer intends to request on the Large Load Customer’s behalf, this information 
forms the Large Load’s initial financial commitment and credit support obligations while the 
customer’s transmission service request is processed and studied by PJM. 
Following completion of PJM’s transmission study and service process, the CRA will be 
updated to reflect any necessary modifications to the load ramp and the dates on which each 
scheduled level of service will become effective, based on the outcome of the PJM study process. 
The resulting information determines the Large Load’s “Firm Load Commitment,” which serves 
as the benchmark for the CRA’s ongoing financial protections.  No less than annually, beginning 
when the Large Load takes service, the applicable Transmission Owner will calculate the 
minimum transmission-revenue contribution associated with the Firm Load Commitment, referred 
to as the Committed Revenue Contribution.  The Transmission Owner will compare that amount 
with qualifying revenues attributable to firm transmission service actually taken to serve the Large 
Load, referred to as the Actual Revenue Contribution.  If the Actual Revenue Contribution is less 
than the Committed Revenue Contribution for such a period (referred to as a “Measurement 
Period”) the Large Load Customer pays only the difference, referred to as the Shortfall Amount. 
Credit support secures the Large Load Customer’s obligations to pay any Shortfall Amount, and 
the CRA’s early- and partial-termination provisions protect the applicable portion of the remaining 
 
30  
Show Cause Order at PP 80-81.

Blazunas and Hammer Direct Joint Testimony at 32-37.

projected revenue contribution if the transmission request or commitment is withdrawn, or the 
Large Load is abandoned or terminated, or its capacity is permanently reduced. 
1. 
The Pro Forma Cost Recovery Agreement and Related Tariff 
Provisions Balance the Timely Integration of Large Loads With 
Protection Against Cost Shifting. 
The Commission preliminarily found that the Tariff may be unjust and unreasonable 
because it lacks adequate mechanisms to mitigate the risk of cost shifting when transmission 
facilities are developed to serve a Large Load that does not materialize or ultimately takes less 
transmission service than anticipated.32  In those circumstances, anticipated transmission revenues 
may not be realized, leaving other transmission customers to bear a greater share of the applicable 
transmission costs.  The Commission, therefore, preliminarily found that the Tariff should include 
a pro forma CRA requiring a minimum contribution toward the relevant Transmission Owner’s 
transmission revenue requirement.33 
The Commission identified several features  such a agreements should contain.34  The 
minimum contribution should be based on the level of jurisdictional transmission service, 
measured in MW, requested on behalf of the Large Load.35  The agreement should also include 
Credit Support or other financial security sufficient to secure the required payment obligations.36  
Payments made under the agreement should be credited toward the applicable Transmission 
Owner’s transmission revenue requirement in accordance with the Commission’s cost-of-service 
regulations, with their formula rate treatment identified in an appropriate workpaper.37 
The Transmission Owners’ proposed CRA adopts those core elements and incorporates 
them into an integrated mechanism that operates over the term of the agreement, which will be a 
minimum of ten years, with the specific term to be negotiated between the applicable Transmission 
Owner and counterparties.   
The proposal, which includes both minimum revenue contribution and financial security 
assurances, while providing flexibility to tailor certain aspects of the CRA to the individual 
Transmission Owner and customer parties, is intended to provide necessary ratepayer protections 
while promoting timely Large Load integration.  The PJM Transmission Owners recognize the 
need to facilitate speed-to-power for Large Load growth, particularly data centers and other 
computational loads driving AI advances and other key technologies.  They have therefore 
established a CRA framework under which projects can advance while their final service quantities 
and timing are determined through the applicable study and service processes.  At the same time,

as the Commission pointed out, existing transmission customers should not be required to absorb 
the revenue consequences of speculative or insufficiently developed projects.  Such projects may  
prompt study, planning, engineering, procurement, or construction activity but do not proceed as 
represented.38 
The CRA balances those interests by requiring a Large Load Customer to make a 
meaningful, financially-secured commitment after completing the applicable Transmission 
Owner’s retail interconnection study process and before proceeding through PJM’s Fast 
Energization Process.  That requirement helps distinguish projects prepared to proceed from less-
developed requests before additional PJM and Transmission Owner resources are devoted to them.  
This approach recognizes and addresses the Commission’s concern that speculative or duplicative 
requests may delay the study process, distort forecasts, result in unnecessary transmission buildout, 
and shift costs when the anticipated load does not materialize. The Commission identified 
meaningful milestones and financial commitments as potential readiness measures that can help 
ensure that Large Load-related transmissions service requests are more viable and more likely to 
proceed as planned.39  The CRA incorporates both measures by tying the Large Load Customer’s 
commitment to defined project milestones and corresponding financial obligations. 
The pro forma CRA included with this filing will not be the exclusive or presumptive 
vehicle for protecting against cost shifting among customers.  Under the PJM Transmission 
Owners’ proposal, Transmission Owners will be permitted to choose between utilizing the pro 
forma CRA or entering into a separate agreement.  Any such separate  agreement, in conjunction 
with applicable federal and state regulatory requirements, must result in minimum transmission 
service and credit standards, or comparable protections, as determined by the Commission.40  This 
flexibility is a key aspect of, and is not severable from, the PJM Transmission Owners’ pro forma 
CRA proposal.  As discussed further below, the PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal also provides 
transparency to state regulators and other interested parties regarding the costs of transmission 
facilities being constructed by the Transmission Owner to provide transmission service on behalf 
of Large Loads.  This transparency will provide state commissions and other retail regulators 
access to information to assist them in allocating some amount of transmission rate costs 
specifically to Large Load classes of customers within their jurisdictions, if desired.  Several PJM 
Transmission Owners also have existing Large Load cost-recovery or ratepayer-protection 
mechanisms already approved by their respective state commissions.  These provisions 
appropriately recognize, as the Commission acknowledged in the Show Cause Order,41 that cost 
recovery agreements and other ratepayer protection mechanisms have been, and continue to be,

utilized at the retail and wholesale levels by utilities within PJM to provide commensurate

2. 
The CRA Assigns Distinct Responsibilities to the Transmission Owner, 
Large Load Customer, and Eligible Customer. 
The Commission’s preliminary finding contemplated a pro forma CRA among PJM, the 
relevant Transmission Owner, and the Eligible Customer taking transmission service on behalf of 
the Large Load, with the Eligible Customer responsible for the minimum contribution and 
associated financial security.43  However, the Commission also acknowledged in the Show Cause 
Order that Transmission Owners have filed agreements under which minimum contributions to 
Commission-jurisdictional transmission revenue requirements are guaranteed directly by Large 
Load Customers, rather than by an Eligible Customer taking transmission service on the Large 
Load Customer’s behalf.44  In order to best account for each entity’s distinct roles in PJM and align 
financial responsibility with control over the risk addressed by the pro forma CRA, the CRA 
proposed herein is structured as a three-party agreement between the applicable Transmission 
Owner, the underlying Large Load (referred to in the CRA as the “Large Load Customer”), and 
the Eligible Customer taking service on behalf of the Large Load Customer.45 
The CRA assigns each Party the obligations corresponding to its role.  The Large Load 
Customer owns, operates, leases, or otherwise controls the Large Load Facilities and therefore 
bears the CRA’s payment and Credit Support obligations.  Those obligations include: (i) providing 
and maintaining Credit Support;46 (ii) paying any Shortfall Amount, Early Termination Amount, 
or Partial Termination Amount;47 (iii) supplying information concerning the Large Load Facilities 
and project status;48 and (iv) remaining responsible for those obligations regardless of a dispute 
with the Eligible Customer.49  The Eligible Customer remains the transmission customer. It 
requests and takes Firm Transmission Service50 under the Tariff on behalf of the Large Load 
Customer, pursues the applicable PJM process, and provides or authorizes access to the service, 
billing, settlement, and allocation information necessary to calculate and verify amounts under the 
CRA.51  The Eligible Customer is not responsible for the Large Load Customer’s payment or

Several Transmission Owners in PJM have processes already established and approved by their state 
commissions.

Page 14 
 
 
 
 
Credit Support obligations.  The Transmission Owner calculates the Committed Revenue 
Contribution, Actual Revenue Contribution, Shortfall Amount, Credit Support Requirement, and 
any applicable termination amount; issues invoices; receives and administers Credit Support; 
performs required reconciliations; and accounts for and credits collected amounts through its 
applicable Commission-jurisdictional rate.  The Eligible Customer is responsible for information-
sharing and administrative functions necessary to implement the CRA, such as providing the 
Transmission Owner billing, settlement, load and allocation information needed to identify 
transmission rate revenues associated with the Large Load Customer.52 
PJM is not a party to the CRA and does not hold or administer CRA Credit Support.  The 
CRA supplements the underlying transmission-service arrangements but does not itself authorize 
transmission service, determine the amount of service available, or independently require 
construction of transmission facilities.  Those matters remain governed by the Tariff, PJM’s study 
and service processes, the applicable transmission-service agreement, and other relevant 
agreements. 
The proposed pro forma CRA’s assignment of direct payment and Credit Support 
obligations to the Large Load Customer, rather than the Eligible Customer, and its exclusion of 
PJM as a contractual counterparty are just and reasonable and consistent with the cooperative 
jurisdictional responsibilities of the Commission and the states contemplated by the FPA.  This 
allocation of obligations more closely aligns financial responsibility with control over the risk 
addressed by the CRA.  The anticipated transmission revenue contribution depends principally on 
whether the Large Load project proceeds, achieves the required readiness, begins taking service at 
the committed level, and continues operating during the CRA term.  The Large Load Customer, 
as the entity that owns, operates, leases, or otherwise controls the Large Load Facilities, is best 
positioned to control those related decisions and circumstances.  The Eligible Customer, by 
contrast, may be an intermediary or unaffiliated load-serving entity that requests and takes 
transmission service on the Large Load Customer’s behalf but does not control the Large Load 
Customer’s construction, financing, readiness, demand, or ongoing operation.   
Requiring the Eligible Customer to secure the Large Load Customer’s revenue 
commitment could therefore place the financial exposure on an entity that does not control whether 
the underlying Large Load Customer develops and operates its project as represented and, at a 
minimum, would require in many cases the implementation and administration of duplicative and 
overlapping financial obligations.  For instance, if the Eligible Customer is an electric distribution 
company, it would need to have in place and maintain appropriate additional agreements to ensure 
that any obligations imposed on it would be assigned to the relevant Large Load, and impose 
adequate security to cover those subsidiary obligations.  This structure could be extremely costly 
(costs that would ultimately be borne by all ratepayers) and, burdensome to administer, particularly 
for those companies acting as Eligible Customers on behalf of multiple Large Loads.  The proposed 
pro forma CRA instead gives the Transmission Owner direct recourse to the real party in interest, 
namely, the load entity whose interconnection drives the need for the protections underlying the 
 
52  
Id. at Article 5.6.

PDF page 15

Honorable Debbie-Anne A. Reese 
November 16, 2026 
Page 15 
 
 
 
 
CRA, while preserving the Eligible Customer’s responsibility for the transmission-service, 
information-sharing, and administrative functions necessary to implement the arrangement. 
This structure is consistent with and respects the jurisdictional boundaries between the 
Commission and state regulators.  The CRA does not eliminate or diminish the requirement that 
transmission service be taken by an Eligible Customer, or otherwise serve to grant, provide or 
otherwise convey transmission service under the Tariff.53  Nor does it establish or govern any 
terms and conditions of retail service.  Rather, the CRA provides a payment and credit support 
mechanism to ensure that the Large Load Customer’s committed transmission-revenue 
contribution is satisfied.  It is the Large Load Customer whose decision to develop and operate the 
Large Load drives the need for the FERC-jurisdictional transmission service requested on its 
behalf, and whose commitments inform the amount of service and associated costs.  It is therefore 
appropriate, and jurisdictionally sound, to place the financial risks addressed by the CRA on the 
Large Load directly.  
This approach accords with the Show Cause Order and Commission precedent.  As noted 
above, the Commission recognized existing agreements under which Large Load Customers 
directly guarantee minimum contributions to Transmission Owners’ Commission-jurisdictional 
transmission revenue requirements.  It requested briefing on whether those agreements could 
satisfy the contemplated CRA requirement where they provide comparable or greater protection, 
including by avoiding duplicative Credit Support and transaction costs.54  The Commission has 
likewise accepted Commission-jurisdictional agreements under which individual Large Load 
Customers directly assumed financial commitments associated with anticipated transmission 
revenues, even though those customers did not themselves take transmission service under the 
Tariff.55   
3. 
The CRA Establishes and Maintains the Service Commitment on 
Which its Financial Protections are Based. 
The CRA identifies the Large Load Customer’s anticipated load demand, proposed load 
ramp, and anticipated timing for each stage of service, upon which the Large Load Customer’s 
financial commitment will be based.56  As part of each Transmission Owner’s or affiliate’s retail 
load interconnection process, the Large Load customer will identify the amount of load it 
anticipates adding to the system along with any ramping schedule for bringing that load online 
over a defined period.  Following completion of the applicable Transmission Owner’s load 
interconnection study process, the Parties execute the CRA based on the amount of transmission 
service that the Eligible Customer will request from PJM on behalf of the Large Load Customer 
in order to accommodate its anticipated demand, including any associated ramping schedule.  
Exhibit A to the CRA records that preliminary information, together with the corresponding

amount and type of Firm Transmission Service that the Eligible Customer intends to request on 
the Large Load Customer’s behalf (the “Initial Load Commitment”), the dates upon which service 
is estimated to begin, and any expected ramp to the project’s full requested load.57  The Initial 
Load Commitment is backed by Credit Support while the PJM transmission service study process 
is pending.  
Execution of the CRA and provision of the required Credit Support are conditions to 
proceeding through the applicable PJM process.58  This financial commitment provides assurance 
that the project is real, deters speculative requests, and protects other transmission customers if the 
Eligible Customer, on behalf of the Large Load Customer, withdraws or abandons the request, the 
Large Load Customer elects to reduce the amount requested, or the Eligible Customer refuses to 
execute the resulting Final PJM Agreement. 
Following completion of the PJM process, the Eligible Customer enters into the Final PJM 
Agreement, which identifies the Firm Transmission Service to be taken on behalf of the Large 
Load Customer.  The Transmission Owner will then update the CRA to reflect the outcome of the 
PJM process.59  If the Final PJM Agreement determines that Firm Transmission Service will not 
be available in order to accommodate a Large Load’s load ramp schedule as reflected in the Initial 
Load Commitment, because necessary IRRs will not be in service at the required times, the CRA 
will be updated accordingly.  The resulting new schedule for bringing the Large Load’s load online 
becomes the “Firm Load Commitment.”  A change to the timing or interim levels of the load ramp 
based on PJM’s determination of when the requested Firm Transmission Service can feasibly be 
provided does not trigger any financial liability for the Large Load Customer.60  By contrast, if the 
Large Load Customer elects to reduce the final amount of Firm Transmission Service requested 
on its behalf during the PJM process (e.g., by reducing the overall size of its facility), that 
customer-elected reduction is subject to the CRA’s partial-termination provisions and payment by 
the Large Load Customer of a portion of the expected revenues associated with the Firm 
Transmission Service that would have been taken to serve its full originally committed load 
amount (the “Partial Termination Amount”).61   
This approach balances the interests of the Large Load Customer and transmission 
customers.  The Large Load Customer is not charged a termination amount merely because PJM 
determines that the requested schedule for obtaining firm transmission service based on its planned 
load ramp is not feasible given the necessary IRRs and their anticipated in-service dates.  At the 
same time, if the Eligible Customer, on behalf of the Large Load Customer, withdraws or abandons 
the Firm Transmission Service request during the PJM process, or refuses to execute the resulting 
Final PJM Agreement, the withdrawal, abandonment, or refusal constitutes an early termination

and requires the Large Load Customer to pay the applicable Early Termination Amount.62  A 
customer-elected reduction in the amount requested constitutes a partial termination and requires 
payment of the applicable Partial Termination Amount.63  This appropriately requires the Large 
Load Customer to make a tangible financial commitment as part of its completion of the 
Transmission Owner’s retail interconnection process, and before entering the PJM process.  Doing 
so discourages speculative and potentially duplicative study requests that would clog the PJM 
process.  It also promotes the efficient use of PJM and Transmission Owner study and planning 
resources by requiring the Large Load Customer to assume the financial consequences of 
withdrawing, abandoning, or voluntarily reducing the request after those processes have begun.  
To be clear, the CRA does not assign the Large Load Customer the costs of particular IRRs 
or a project-specific share of the IRR portfolio. The CRA and Schedule 12, which as discussed 
below establishes the cost allocation for IRRs, serve distinct functions: Schedule 12 governs the 
allocation and recovery of the revenue requirements associated with IRRs, while the CRA 
establishes the minimum Firm Transmission Service-related revenue contribution associated with 
the Large Load Customer’s commitment.64 
4. 
Credit Support Gives Financial Substance to the Large Load 
Customer’s Commitment. 
As discussed above, the proposed pro forma CRA commits the Large Load Customer to a 
transmission-revenue contribution and related payment obligations based on the level of 
transmission service.  To ensure that obligation, the Commission identified credit support as an 
important component of the contemplated cost recovery agreement.65  To implement that 
requirement, the CRA requires the Large Load Customer to provide Credit Support shortly after 
the Parties execute the agreement.66  Under proposed Tariff, Part VIII, Subpart N, section 441, 
execution of a CRA, or a qualifying alternative agreement, and provision of the required security 
are conditions to proceeding through the applicable PJM Fast Energization Cycle and obtaining 
Firm Transmission Service.67 
This sequencing gives financial weight to the Large Load Customer’s preliminary 
commitment before additional studies are performed by PJM and development activity occurs.

Page 18 
 
 
 
 
The CRA helps cement those Large Load Customers that are prepared to assume the financial 
consequences of their requested service, while giving customers advance notice through the pro 
forma CRA of the obligations required to proceed.  The initial Credit Support Requirement is based 
on the service amount and timing reflected in the CRA at execution.  More specifically, the Initial 
Credit Support Requirement equals the aggregate projected charges associated with 100 percent 
of the Initial Load Commitment over the CRA term.68  Following execution of the Final PJM 
Agreement, the Transmission Owner recalculates the requirement using 100 percent of the 
projected charges associated with the updated Firm Load Commitment and remaining Service 
Dates.69   
As discussed in the Blazunas and Hammer Direct Joint Testimony, over the term of the 
CRA, the Large Load Customer’s credit support requirement is adjusted to reflect its obligations 
as those remaining obligations evolve.70  As each Measurement Period is completed, the revenues 
attributable to service for the Large Load Customer and any resulting Shortfall Amount are 
determined.71  Once the Large Load Customer has satisfied the obligations for that period, the 
amounts of those obligations are subtracted from the Credit Support calculation.72  The secured 
amount therefore declines as the underlying exposure is resolved rather than remaining fixed at its 
initial level throughout the CRA term.  Projected future transmission-service revenues do not 
reduce Credit Support before those revenues are realized.  The Credit Support protects against the 
failure of those anticipated revenues to materialize, which would be detrimental to other customers.  
The Credit Support remains as collateral unless and until it is applied to an amount actually due.  
A draw made only to preserve the value of an expiring or nonconforming letter of credit does not 
itself constitute payment or revenue.  A draw for nonpayment is limited to the amount due and 
unpaid.73   
5. 
Early and Partial Termination Payments Protect the Commitment if 
the Project Exits or the Firm Load Commitment is Reduced. 
As further discussed in the Blazunas and Hammer Direct Joint Testimony, Credit Support 
secures the Large Load Customer’s obligations, including amounts payable if the project 
terminates or the Large Load Customer elects to reduce permanently the amount of Firm 
Transmission Service requested on its behalf or, after execution of the Final PJM Agreement, the 
Firm Load Commitment before the end of the CRA term.74  Before execution of the Final PJM 
Agreement, withdrawal or abandonment of the Firm Transmission Service request, refusal to

execute the resulting agreement, or a reduction in the requested service may trigger an Early 
Termination Amount or Partial Termination Amount.75   
After execution of the Final PJM Agreement, voluntary termination, permanent reduction 
of the Firm Load Commitment, abandonment or cessation of the project, specified readiness 
failures, or an uncured default may trigger the corresponding payment obligation.76  A 
modification to the timing or interim levels of the load ramp resulting from PJM’s determination 
of when the requested Firm Transmission Service can feasibly be provided does not constitute a 
Partial Termination.  Failure to achieve the readiness necessary to take the applicable Firm 
Transmission Service within the period specified in the CRA may constitute a Partial Termination 
or, following the initial Service Date, support termination based on project abandonment or 
cessation.77  The Early Termination Amount protects a specified portion of the revenue 
contribution projected for the remainder of the CRA term: 
Early Termination Amount = (Applicable Termination Percentage × Present 
Value of Remaining Projected Committed Revenue Contributions) − 
Applicable Credits 
The Applicable Termination Percentage increases as the project advances.78  Under the 
current proposal, the percentage is 25 percent before execution of the Final PJM Agreement, 50 
percent during the first 180 days after execution, 75 percent thereafter until the first Service Date, 
and 100 percent on and after the first Service Date.79  This graduated increase in financial 
obligations gives the initial commitment economic substance, while recognizing that early-stage 
withdrawal or change in load commitment is likely to have fewer detrimental impacts on other 
customers and the overall efficiency of the process, as compared to terminations that occur after 
the end of PJM’s process and closer to the Large Load’s planned in-service date.80  It also reflects 
the increasing reliance that may be placed on the Large Load Customer and its anticipated revenue 
contribution as Transmission Owners undertake engineering, procurement, construction, and other 
development activities with respect to the IRRs needed to serve the Large Load Customer and 
other customers in the applicable PJM study cluster. 
The termination provisions serve an important project-readiness function. They are 
appropriately not limited to the recovery of costs that can be traced to particular transmission 
facilities or stranded investments.  By requiring a Large Load Customer to commit to a meaningful 
financial stake as the project advances, the termination provisions also discourage speculative or 
insufficiently developed requests from entering and remaining in the transmission service process. 
This structure advances the Commission’s objective of using meaningful financial commitments

to improve the viability of Large Load-related transmission service requests and reduce the 
potential for speculative requests to consume study and planning resources or distort transmission 
planning assumptions.  It also reduces the potential that speculative requests will adversely affect 
other Large Load-related transmission service requests and their ability to receive service in a 
timely manner.81 
Payment of a Partial Termination Amount applies only to the portion of the commitment 
permanently reduced.82  Amounts already received or applied to the same prospective exposure, 
including qualifying Credit Support proceeds and identifiable replacement-load revenues, reduce 
the termination amount.  The termination obligations are therefore protective rather than punitive: 
they are tied to the remaining projected revenue contribution, adjusted according to the Large Load 
Customer’s stage in the process, and reduced by amounts already recovered or otherwise 
attributable to the same exposure. 
As discussed in Section 10 below, proposed CRA section 9.12 and proposed Tariff, Part 
VIII, Subpart N, section 441(e) govern the application and distribution of termination amounts 
collected before the first Service Date. 
6. 
Service Dates Determine When the Periodic Revenue Commitment 
Begins. 
If the Large Load Customer proceeds, each portion of the recurring revenue commitment 
begins on the corresponding Service Date identified in CRA, Exhibit A.  For an authorized load 
ramp included in the CRA, the Committed Revenue Contribution increases as the scheduled stages 
of the Firm Load Commitment become effective.  The CRA also accounts for circumstances in 
which Firm Transmission Service cannot begin because necessary transmission facilities have not 
been completed.83  If the delay is not caused by the Large Load Customer’s or Eligible Customer’s 
failure or lack of reasonable diligence, the affected Service Date and corresponding contribution 
are extended until the service becomes available.84  If a qualifying transmission-facility delay 
reaches the threshold specified in the CRA, the Large Load Customer may accept revising the 
timing to ramp up its load or terminate under the CRA’s limited delay provision.85  The qualifying 
delay period is excluded when determining the applicable termination milestone so that the Large 
Load Customer does not move into a higher termination percentage solely because of a delay it 
did not cause.

7. 
The CRA Establishes a Minimum Revenue Contribution Based on the 
Firm Load Commitment. 
The Early and Partial Termination Amounts discussed above address the remaining 
projected revenue exposure if the Large Load Customer exits or permanently reduces its Firm 
Load Commitment.  By contrast, if the Large Load Customer proceeds, the Committed Revenue 
Contribution defines the minimum periodic transmission-revenue contribution required under the 
CRA once the applicable Service Dates begin.86  Beginning on the applicable Service Dates, the 
Transmission Owner determines the revenues that would be associated with Firm Transmission 
Service corresponding to the Firm Load Commitment and applies the Minimum Commitment 
Percentage specified in Exhibit A.  The resulting amount is the Committed Revenue Contribution: 
Committed Revenue Contribution = Minimum Commitment Percentage × 
Full Firm Transmission Service Benchmark87 
The Minimum Commitment Percentage may vary between 80 and 100 percent, and may 
change over the CRA term under a schedule identified in Exhibit A.88  As discussed in the Joint 
Blazunas and Hammer Direct Joint Testimony, the proposed Minimum Commitment Percentage 
is reasonable because it establishes “a meaningful minimum revenue commitment while allowing 
individual Transmission Owners to require a higher commitment where appropriate based on their 
particular circumstances.”89  Together with the authorized load ramp and applicable Service Dates, 
this flexibility allows the Committed Revenue Contribution to reflect the Large Load’s individual 
circumstances, including its expected progression and the timing of the associated transmission-
revenue exposure.  This structure implements the Commission’s preliminary view that the 
minimum contribution should be related to the MW level of jurisdictional transmission service 
requested on behalf of the Large Load, while accommodating the request’s progression toward full 
service.90 
8. 
The CRA Recognizes Revenues Actually Produced as a Result of the 
Transmission Service Provided. 
The Committed Revenue Contribution establishes the minimum periodic contribution 
required under the CRA.91  For each Measurement Period, the Transmission Owner compares that 
amount with the Actual Revenue Contribution, which consists of the qualifying revenues 
attributable to Firm Transmission Service actually taken by the Eligible Customer to serve the 
Large Load Customer.92  The Committed Revenue Contribution and the revenues credited against

it are calculated using corresponding categories of transmission charges.93  This ensures a 
consistent comparison and gives the Large Load Customer credit for all qualifying revenues 
corresponding to the charges included in that calculation. 
Because the Eligible Customer takes the underlying transmission service, it must provide 
or authorize access to the billing, settlement, load, and allocation information needed to identify 
the revenues attributable to the Large Load Customer.94  That information-sharing obligation 
allows the Transmission Owner to administer the CRA while preserving the Large Load 
Customer’s separate responsibility for the financial obligations.  The CRA also accommodates 
existing retail arrangements.  To the extent the Large Load Customer makes separate payments 
through a retail tariff or other retail mechanism that reflect the assignment of Firm Transmission 
Service or related costs to the Large Load Customer, those payments are taken into account in 
determining the Actual Revenue Contribution, insofar as doing so does not shift costs to other 
Eligible Customers taking service in the Transmission Owner’s Zone.95  This treatment recognizes 
qualifying retail payments without permitting the associated transmission costs to be shifted to 
other customers. 
9. 
Under the CRA, the Large Load Customer Pays Only the Resulting 
Shortfall. 
At the end of each Measurement Period, the Transmission Owner compares the qualifying 
revenues attributable to the Large Load Facilities with the Committed Revenue Contribution: 
Shortfall Amount = Committed Revenue Contribution − Actual Revenue 
Contribution 
If recognized revenues equal or exceed the committed amount, no Shortfall Amount is due 
under the CRA.96  If recognized revenues are lower, the Large Load Customer pays only the 
difference.97  The Shortfall Amount is therefore a true-up of the agreed minimum contribution, not 
a second charge for the same Firm Transmission Service.  The Eligible Customer continues to pay 
the charges imposed under the Tariff for service actually taken, and the qualifying revenues 
produced by that service are credited against the Committed Revenue Contribution before any 
CRA payment is calculated.  
This mechanism protects other transmission customers by ensuring receipt of the minimum 
contribution associated with the Large Load commitment.98  The Large Load Customer receives

both ordinary transmission-service charges and a duplicative CRA payment for the same 
contribution. Although the mechanism is principally intended to protect other transmission 
customers from cost shifting, the CRA includes procedural and substantive safeguards to ensure 
that the Large Load Customer pays no more than the amount properly due.  The Transmission 
Owner must support any invoiced Shortfall Amount, and the Large Load Customer may review 
and dispute the calculation.99  Payment and Credit Support obligations remain in effect during the 
dispute, but any overpayment or excess draw must be returned after the dispute is resolved. 
10. 
Pre-Service Date Termination Amounts are Shared Among Affected 
Transmission Owners, and Collected Amounts are Credited Without 
Duplicate Recovery. 
The Commission stated that CRA payments should reduce the applicable Transmission 
Owner’s transmission revenue requirement consistent with the Commission’s cost-of-service 
regulations and contemplated a formula rate workpaper showing that treatment.100  Consistent with 
that guidance, collected Shortfall Amounts reduce the applicable transmission revenue 
requirement and remain subject to the Transmission Owner’s formula rate protocols and 
Commission review.101  Credit Support remains collateral unless and until it is applied to an 
amount due. 
CRA section 9.12 provides that, if an Early Termination Amount or Partial Termination 
Amount is payable before the first Service Date, the collected amount is first applied to Project 
Development Costs in accordance with proposed Tariff, Part VIII, Subpart N, section 441(e).  This 
treatment recognizes that facilities needed to serve a Large Load may be cancelled or otherwise 
abandoned as a result of Early or Partial Termination by one or more Large Load Customers.  
Moreover, because multiple Transmission Owners may be affected by such IRR cancellations or 
abandonments resulting from the termination of a Large Load Customer, Early or Partial 
Termination by a Large Load Customer could result in the cancellation of projects not only by the 
Transmission Owner that is the signatory to the applicable CRA, but also by other Transmission 
Owners within PJM.   
Proposed Tariff, Part VIII, Subpart N, section 441(e) addresses this situation by providing 
a mechanism for the distribution of  Partial and Early Termination Amounts among the contracting 
Transmission Owner and other eligible Transmission Owners based on their respective pro rata 
shares of the applicable Eligible Costs, if any, which are derived from costs reported on the 
applicable Transmission Owner’s FERC Form 1.102  This sharing mechanism prevents the entire

Proposed pro forma CRA, Part IX, Subpart P at Articles 7.4, 7.5.

Eligible Costs are defined as all expenditures, costs, and cancellation fees incurred or due to commitments 
made by the third-party Transmission Owner to engineer, design, procure, construct, own, operate, maintain, and 
service the Interconnection Reliability Reinforcements needed to serve the Eligible Customer taking transmission 
service on behalf of the Eligible Load or Large Load, but which will no longer be constructed, which (a) are incurred

PDF page 24

Honorable Debbie-Anne A. Reese

Page 24 
 
 
 
 
payment from remaining with the contracting Transmission Owner while other affected 
Transmission Zones continue to bear costs associated with IRRs no longer needed.  Any amount 
remaining after the application of Termination Amounts to Eligible Costs of the applicable 
Transmission Owner is credited to the contracting Transmission Owner’s transmission revenue 
requirement to the benefit of other customers in the Zone.  If termination occurs on or after the 
first Service Date, the applicable termination amount is credited to the Transmission Owner’s 
Transmission Revenue Requirement for the Transmission Zone in which the Eligible Customer 
would have taken Firm Transmission Service.  The different treatment reflects the change in the 
nature of the protected exposure.  Before the first Service Date, termination may result in Eligible 
Costs associated with the cancellation or abandonment of facilities being constructed by 
Transmission Owners; after service begins, the termination amount solely protects other customers 
in the amount of the remaining prospective revenue contribution under the CRA, which is tied to 
the contracting Transmission Owner’s zonal transmission revenue requirement.  In neither case do 
Transmission Owners benefit financially from the allocation of Termination Amounts. 
The CRA and proposed Tariff provisions contain additional protections against double 
recovery.103 Actual revenues reduce periodic Shortfall Amounts; amounts already applied to the 
same exposure reduce termination obligations; qualifying retail and replacement-load revenues are 
recognized where appropriate; and collateral is not treated as revenue until applied.  In addition, a 
Transmission Owner may not seek recovery through an abandoned-plant filing or another 
proceeding for amounts already received through the Tariff, Part VIII, Subpart N, section 441(e) 
distribution process.  Together, these provisions ensure that CRA recoveries follow the associated 
exposure across affected Transmission Owners without providing duplicative recovery. 
11. 
The Proposed Pro Forma CRA and Generic Tariff Provisions Provide 
a Transparent and Flexible Cost Recovery Framework. 
Proposed Tariff, Part VIII, Subpart N, sections 441(c) and (d) establish uniform procedures 
and deadlines for tendering, negotiating, executing, securing, and filing the CRA.  Filing the CRA 
as a pro forma agreement gives Large Load Customers and Eligible Customers advance notice of 
the commitment, Credit Support, shortfall, termination, crediting, and information-sharing 
obligations that will apply when a Large Load-related transmission service request proceeds 
through PJM’s Fast Energization Process, while permitting the project-specific terms 
contemplated by the form to reflect differences in service, timing, load ramp, and applicable rates. 
As discussed above, the Tariff also permits existing or separate agreements to satisfy the 
CRA requirement where they provide comparable protection.  Proposed section 441(a)(ii) permits 
a separate Commission-accepted agreement that, together with applicable federal and state 
requirements, establishes minimum Firm Transmission Service and credit standards or other 
 
through the end of the calendar year in which notice of termination was received from the contracting Transmission 
Owner, and (b) are included in amounts reported in the Transmission Owner’s FERC Form 1 covering that calendar 
year.  See proposed Tariff, Part VIII, Subpart N, section 441(e)(ii).

See Proposed Tariff, Part VIII, Subpart N, section 441(e); see also proposed pro forma CRA, Part IX, 
Subpart P at Exhibit D.

PDF page 25

Honorable Debbie-Anne A. Reese

comparable protections against cost shifting.  Section 441(b) preserves qualifying existing 
agreements, and section 441(e)(v) requires alternative agreements to contain reasonably 
comparable termination-payment crediting provisions.   
The proposal also expressly preserves qualifying existing agreements.104  Where a Large 
Load, including an anticipated load ramp, is already covered by a qualifying existing agreement, 
that agreement and the commitments reflected in it need not be replaced by the pro forma CRA 
merely because the new CRA framework becomes effective.  The grandfathering provision thus 
preserves arrangements and commitments already in place under the existing framework. 
12. 
The Proposed CRA is Just and Reasonable and Not Unduly 
Discriminatory or Preferential. 
The CRA is just and reasonable because it implements the protections identified by the 
Commission while accommodating the practical requirements of Large Load development.105  It 
permits projects to advance through the applicable load interconnection study and transmission 
service processes while establishing, at appropriate milestones, a defined and financially secured 
commitment corresponding to the Firm Transmission Service requested on their behalf.  This 
structure supports the timely integration of viable Large Loads without requiring existing 
transmission customers to fully bear the revenue consequences if a project does not proceed as 
represented or takes less service than anticipated. 
The CRA also assigns responsibilities according to the parties’ respective roles.  The Large 
Load Customer assumes the payment and Credit Support obligations associated with the project it 
controls.  The Eligible Customer remains responsible for requesting and taking transmission 
service and providing the information necessary to administer the agreement.  The Transmission 
Owner calculates and reconciles the applicable amounts, administers Credit Support, and credits 
collected amounts through its Commission-jurisdictional rate.  PJM continues to administer the 
transmission-service study process.  This allocation places each obligation with the entity best 
positioned to perform it without altering the established responsibilities for authorizing service or 
planning and constructing transmission facilities. 
The Large Load Customer’s financial commitment is also adjusted as the project develops. 
Before completion of PJM’s process, the CRA is based on the Initial Load Commitment and 
secures the consequences of advancing the request. Following execution of the Final PJM 
Agreement, the commitment is conformed to the amount and timing of Firm Transmission Service 
established through PJM’s process.  The Credit Support Requirement is likewise recalculated, but 
it remains based on 100 percent of the projected full-service benchmark for the remaining term 
rather than on the Minimum Commitment Percentage or projected future actual revenues.  If 
service begins, the Large Load Customer receives credit for qualifying revenues attributable to 
Firm Transmission Service actually taken to serve the Large Load Customer and pays only the 
resulting Shortfall Amount.  If the project exits or is permanently reduced, the applicable 
 
104  
Proposed Tariff, Part VIII, Subpart N, section 441(b). 
105  
Blazunas and Hammer Direct Joint Testimony at 43.

PDF page 26

Honorable Debbie-Anne A. Reese 
November 16, 2026 
Page 26 
 
 
 
 
termination obligation addresses the remaining prospective exposure based on the stage reached 
by the project and the amounts already recovered. 
These protections support efficient transmission development while protecting customers 
in the precise fashion the Commission suggested in the Show Cause Order.106  Requiring a defined 
commitment and Credit Support at identified stages gives PJM and the PJM Transmission Owners 
a more reliable basis for devoting study, planning, engineering, procurement, and construction 
resources to Large Load-related transmission service requests.  At the same time, conforming the 
commitment to PJM’s final service determination, recognizing qualifying actual and retail 
revenues, reducing Credit Support as obligations are resolved, and preventing duplicate recovery 
protect the Large Load Customer against obligations exceeding the exposure addressed by the 
CRA.  Although the amount of Credit Support may exceed the periodic or termination amount 
ultimately payable because it is calculated using 100 percent of the projected benchmark, Credit 
Support remains collateral and does not determine or increase the underlying payment obligation. 
The filed framework is also not unduly discriminatory or preferential.  The pro forma CRA 
establishes the same core commitment, security, shortfall, termination, crediting, and information-
sharing requirements for similarly situated Large Loads.  Differences in service quantities, load 
ramps, Service Dates, calculation periods, applicable rates, and Credit Support reflect relevant 
differences in the service requested, project timing, and the applicable Transmission Owner’s rate 
structure.  Existing or separate agreements may substitute for the pro forma CRA only where they 
provide comparable protection against cost shifting, including appropriate service commitments, 
financial security, and treatment of termination payments.  
Taken together, these provisions respond directly to the Commission’s concerns while 
recognizing the scale and pace of Large Load development in PJM.  They provide a workable 
framework for advancing projects prepared to make and secure the commitments on which 
transmission planning and investment will rely, while protecting existing transmission customers 
if the anticipated Firm Transmission Service contribution does not materialize.  The resulting 
allocation of obligations is proportionate to the risks addressed, consistent with the parties’ 
respective roles, and reasonably designed to support both Large Load integration and efficient 
transmission infrastructure development.

B. 
Transmission Service Rates and Related Charges for Co-Located Load and 
Large Load 
1. 
The Filing Satisfies the Remaining Co-Located Load Rate Compliance 
Directives. 
a. 
Transmission Services. 
The Co-Located Load Paper Hearing Order found the PJM Transmission Owners’ rate 
treatment proposals for FCDS and Interim NITS just and reasonable.107  However, the Commission 
rejected the PJM Transmission Owners’ proposed NFCDS maximum rate and required a lower 
maximum rate or nondiscriminatory discounts supported by transparent calculations.  
Accordingly, the PJM Transmission Owners’ filing implements the rate treatments already found 
just and reasonable for FCDS and Interim NITS through compliance tariff records,108 and proposes 
a revised NFCDS discounted rate methodology and related tariff provisions in compliance with 
the Commission’s directives.109 
 
The Co-Located Load Paper Hearing Order also found the Tariff unjust and unreasonable 
because it does not include transmission services that reflect Eligible Customers taking 
transmission service on behalf of loads with behind-the-meter generation (“BTMG”) that are 
willing and able to limit their energy withdrawals from the transmission system under certain 
conditions.110  The Commission directed (i) PJM to revise its Tariff to extend the three new 
transmission services set forth in the December 18 Show Cause Order to Eligible Customers taking 
service on behalf of load with BTMG that are willing and able to limit their energy withdrawals 
from the transmission system under certain conditions, and (ii) the PJM Transmission Owners to 
reflect this change in their compliance filing.111  Accordingly, the PJM Transmission Owners 
acknowledge in this compliance filing the extension of FCDS, NFCDS, and Interim NITS to 
Eligible Customers taking service on behalf of load with BTMG (i.e., Eligible Load)112 that 
includes necessary control technologies and/or protection systems that limit their energy 
withdrawals from the transmission system under certain conditions.

2. 
Financial Penalties for Unreserved Transmission System Use. 
In the Co-Located Load Paper Hearing Order, the Commission determined that it is just 
and reasonable to impose financial penalties on Eligible Customers in any circumstance where the 
transmission customer uses a transmission service that it has not reserved.113  The Commission 
directed the PJM Transmission Owners to effectuate on compliance their proposed penalty 
framework for financial penalties for Eligible Customers taking one of the three new transmission 
services that withdraw from the transmission system in excess of their contract demand level (i.e., 
for unreserved use).114  The Commission also noted that, on compliance, the PJM Transmission 
Owners’ unreserved use penalties should be distributed to all non-offending transmission 
customers, not just Eligible Customers taking Interim NITS and FCDS, consistent with Order No. 
890.115  Accordingly, the PJM Transmission Owners’ filing implements the financial penalty 
framework the Commission found just and reasonable, including the distribution of revenues 
collected from financial penalty charges to Eligible Customers taking NFCDS, in addition to firm 
customers.116 
C. 
Extension of Transmission Services and Financial Penalties for Unreserved 
Transmission System use to Eligible Large Load 
In the Show Cause Order, the Commission asked PJM to explain whether the Tariff 
remains just and reasonable if the three new services for Co-Located Load customers (i.e., Interim 
NITS, FCDS, and NFCDS) are not also made available to Eligible Customers taking service on 
behalf of flexible and electrically proximate Large Loads.117 
In its filing on compliance with the Show Cause Order, PJM is extending the same three 
transmission services to Eligible Customers taking transmission service on behalf of Large Load 
customers, as it offers to Eligible Customers taking transmission service on behalf of Co-Located 
Load customers in Docket Nos. EL25-49, et al., with substantially the same requirements for 
protective systems and operational compliance.118   
Consistent with this approach, the PJM Transmission Owners propose, pursuant to FPA 
section 205, to apply to Eligible Customers taking these transmission services on behalf of Large 
Load customers the same corresponding rate and financial penalty frameworks that apply to 
Eligible Customers taking transmission service on behalf of Co-Located Load customers,

1. 
Firm Contract Demand Transmission Service Rates and Charges. 
The PJM Transmission Owners’ proposed rates for FCDS are identical to those set forth in 
the Transmission Owners’ reply brief in Docket Nos. EL25-49-000, et al.120  The rate for FCDS 
will be calculated in a manner essentially identical to the existing NITS rates, using each applicable 
transmission owner’s specific methodology for NITS customers.121  Under this approach, Eligible 
Customers taking FCDS will pay the same base charge per megawatt (“MW”) as NITS customers, 
except that unlike NITS customers their demand will be their specified firm contract demand equal 
to the firm contract demand amount (in MW) multiplied by a single zonal rate for both NITS and 
FCDS customers.  The Commission agreed with this approach in the Co-Located Load Paper 
Hearing Order, finding it just and reasonable to base the Firm Contract Demand rate on zonal NITS 
rates and underlying NITS revenue requirements because both services provide a similar level of
