For Zones in which NITS rates are determined pursuant to Attachment H formula rates, 
the monthly demand charge is calculated as follows:  MDC = FCDTSA × RTZ/12, where MDC is 
the monthly demand charge, FCDTSA is the Firm Contract Demand Transmission Service 
Amount in MW as specified in the Eligible Customer’s Service Agreement, and RTZ is the 
applicable zonal NITS rate stated in dollars per MW-year.123  For purposes of establishing RTZ 
under Attachment H, the annual peak of the zone shall include both Network Customer Loads and 
the FCDS amounts for all Eligible Customers taking FCDS in the zone during the applicable peak 
period.124   
Consistent with the Commission’s findings in the Co-Located Load Paper Hearing Order, 
all ancillary services other than regulation and black start will be charged based on the Eligible 
Customer’s contract demand, while regulation and black start services will be assessed on a gross 
demand basis—reflecting the Commission’s determination that all Co-Located Loads rely on those 
services to be served regardless of the extent to which they withdraw energy from the transmission 
system.125  PJM will procure capacity for Firm Contract Demand customers and will plan the 
transmission system to serve them at their contract demand level.126  Accordingly, capacity-related 
charges will be based on the customer’s Unforced Capacity Obligation.  Eligible Customers will 
also be responsible for applicable administrative service charges, redispatch costs under 
Attachment K, and Transmission Enhancement Charges for which they are designated as 
Responsible Customers under Schedule 12.127 
Revenue credits from NFCDS revenues will be applied each month to offset gross Firm 
Contract Demand charges on a pro rata basis, consistent with the crediting mechanisms previously 
approved in PJM’s Tariff.128  FCDS has a minimum one-year term and is subject to PJM’s anti-
toggling mechanism—which prohibits, for a period of five years, an Eligible Customer from taking 
NFCDS for any portion of Co-Located Load served by FCDS—and a 42-month termination-notice 
requirement, consistent with the notice provisions applicable to Network Customers.129

2. 
Non-Firm Contract Demand Transmission Service Rates and Charges. 
In the Co-Located Load Paper Hearing Order, the Commission found that the PJM 
Transmission Owners’ original proposal to set the maximum Non-Firm Contract Demand rate 
equal to the Firm Contract Demand rate had not been shown to be just and reasonable,  
notwithstanding that it was subject to discount similar to Non-Firm Point-To-Point Service.130   
The Commission concluded that Non-Firm Contract Demand should not be subject to the same 
maximum rate as Firm Contract Demand.131  The Commission further directed the PJM 
Transmission Owners to propose either a lower maximum rate or non-discriminatory discounts, 
with transparent charge calculations for each individual transmission customer.132 
In compliance with the Commission’s directive, and as supported by the Blazunas and 
Hammer Direct Joint Testimony, the PJM Transmission Owners are proposing to set the NFCDS 
rate as a discounted rate established as a function of the zonal FCDS rate and the transmission 
system load factor for a zone.133  NFCDS customers will take service and pay their reserved 
amount of demand based on system availability, and NFCDS will be available for terms ranging 
from one hour to one month.134  To ensure against double recovery, all revenues received from 
NFCDS charges will be credited to NITS, Firm Point-To-Point, and FCDS customers based on 
their share of the coincident peak load. 
The rationale for this approach is that the transmission system is built to a peak driven by 
firm load.  As discussed in the Joint Blazunas and Hammer testimony, the difference between 
zonal annual energy and zonal annual energy at a 100% load factor represents a proxy for the 
“excess capacity” for which non-firm load is not responsible.135  Accordingly, a lower zonal load 
factor indicates relatively more spare capacity and justifies a higher discount, and vice versa.  A 
simplified example of this rate calculation for a zone is set forth below in Example 2: 
Example 2 – Discounted Non-Firm Contract Demand Transmission Service Rate 
Calculation 
 
1  The zonal Firm Contract Demand Rate is $90,000/MW-Year 
2  The zonal peak demand is 150,000/MW. 
3  The zonal annual energy is 850,000,000 MWh 
4  The maximum non-firm contract demand equals $90,000/MW-Year x [Line 3 ÷ (Line 2 
x 8,760 hours)] = $90,000/MW-Year x 64.7% Load Factor ≈ $58,219/MW-Year.

The PJM Transmission Owners’ proposed discounted NFCDS rate is just and reasonable 
and consistent with cost causation principles, as well as the Commission’s directives in the 
December 18 Show Cause Order and Co-Located Load Paper Hearing Order because it 
incentivizes efficient use of the transmission system by Eligible Customers seeking to use the 
transmission system on behalf of Co-Located or Large Load in a more limited manner and aligns 
the charges for NFCDS with the benefits received.137  This methodology is also transparent and 
non-discriminatory, as it relies on publicly available PJM zonal data, such as peak loads and net 
energy figures from PJM’s Long-Term Load Forecast, and applies a consistent calculation across 
all PJM zones.138   
3. 
Interim Network Integration Transmission Service. 
On August 17, 2026, the PJM Transmission Owners submitted new and revised Tariff 
records in partial compliance with the Commission’s Co-Located Load Order to implement the 
rate for Interim NITS.139  The filing was a companion filing to PJM’s August 17 Compliance Filing 
of the Tariff terms and conditions for Interim NITS.140   
The PJM Transmission Owners propose limited modifications to the language contained 
in their August 17 Compliance Filing to account for the provision of this service to Eligible 
Customers taking service on behalf of Large Load customers,141 as well as to reflect 
implementation of FCDS and NFCDS. 
Interim NITS is a temporary, non-firm transmission service available to an Eligible 
Customer that has requested NITS but cannot yet reliably receive it because the Network Upgrades 
necessary to provide permanent NITS are not yet complete.142  The Commission found that Interim 
NITS is an optional service, available only to the extent PJM determines—in consultation with the 
affected Transmission Owner—that some level of interim service can be reliably provided based 
on the results of appropriate studies.143  Once the required Network Upgrades are completed and 
integrated with the PJM transmission system, the Eligible Customer automatically converts to

As with FCDS, the PJM Transmission Owners’ proposed rates for Interim NITS are the 
same as those applicable to Co-Located Load customers under the PJM Transmission Owners’ 
filings in Docket Nos. EL25-49, et al.145  The monthly demand charge for Interim NITS is 
calculated as the Eligible Customer’s measured demand amount in MW multiplied by the 
applicable zonal NITS rate established pursuant to Tariff Section 34.1.146  This is the same rate 
methodology that applies to permanent NITS customers, consistent with the Commission’s finding 
that Eligible Customers taking Interim NITS should pay the NITS rate.147  However, because 
Interim NITS is an interruptible service, Eligible Customers taking Interim NITS service on behalf 
of Co-Located Loads or Large Loads would not be assessed generation capacity charges because 
capacity will not be procured on behalf of Interim NITS customers until such time as Network 
Upgrades are in place to serve them.148 
Interim NITS customers will be responsible to pay for: (i) applicable administrative service 
charges under Tariff Schedules 1A, 9, and 10; (ii) ancillary service charges, including regulation 
charges and black start service charges; and (iii) redispatch costs.149  The PJM Transmission 
Owners are not proposing tariff revisions in this filing to implement these charges and costs 
because the Commission directed PJM to incorporate Tariff revisions in its compliance filing 
necessary to assess them.150  Revenue credits from NFCDS revenues will be applied to offset the 
gross Interim NITS monthly demand charge, consistent with the crediting methodology applicable 
to other firm and non-firm service customers.151

framework, an Eligible Customer would be assessed: (i) a base penalty charge for each hour in

which the Eligible Customer’s reserved service limit is exceeded at the Point of Interconnection 
during at least one five-minute interval (an “Exceedance Hour”), and (ii) a penalty ratchet when 
exceedances occur repeatedly.152  Under the penalty ratchet, penalty charges escalate as the 
frequency of unreserved transmission use increases.153  If more than one Exceedance Hour occurs 
on the same day, an additional daily penalty would apply.154  If exceedances occur on multiple 
days within the same week, an additional weekly penalty would apply.  If exceedances occur in 
multiple weeks within the same month, an additional monthly penalty would apply.  Finally, if 
exceedances occur in multiple months within the same year, an additional annual penalty would 
apply.155  The Commission agreed with PJM Transmission Owners that progressively increasing 
the consequences of repeated penalties encourages Eligible Customers to manage their usage 
consistent with their reserved transmission service limit.156 
Consistent with the Commission’s guidance and Order No. 890,157 the PJM Transmission 
Owners are also modifying Schedule 22 to clarify that revenues collected from daily, weekly, and 
monthly financial penalty charges will be returned on a monthly basis to all non-offending 
transmission customers (i.e., FCDS, NFCDS, NITS, and Interim NITS customers) that have not 
been penalized during the month as bill credits, based on the Eligible Customer’s pro rata share 
of the Zone’s annual peak load based on the most recent 12-month period ending October 31.  
Revenues collected from annual penalty charges will be returned on an annual basis to all non-
offending transmission customers that have not been penalized during the year as bill credit, based 
on each Eligible Customer’s pro rata share of the Zone’s annual peak load based on the most 
recent 12-month period ending October 31. 
D. 
Cost Allocation and Recovery for Interconnection Reliability Reinforcements 
PJM’s proposed Fast Energization Process would study qualifying generation  
interconnection and Large Load-related transmission service requests together and identify a 
portfolio of transmission upgrades—the IRRs—needed to address the combined system impacts 
of the requests included in each study cycle.  PJM’s proposal necessitates revisions to the Tariff to 
establish the basis for allocating the costs of the IRRs that PJM identifies in each study cycle.  
Accordingly, the PJM Transmission Owners propose revisions to Schedule 12 of the Tariff that 
would allocate the costs of those IRRs on a portfolio basis using modeled electrical impacts of the 
generators and Large-Load-related transmission service requests being studied through PJM’s Fast

Page 35 
 
 
 
 
Energization process.158  The IRRs themselves will be included in the transmission rate bases of 
the Transmission Owners constructing and owning the projects.  A new Interconnection Reliability 
Reinforcement Charge will be implemented to recover the costs from transmission customers 
external to the Zone(s) in which the IRRs are located, as well as a separate fee to be charged to 
generators in each cycle to recover and credit back to load customers amounts representing a fair 
contribution from those generators to offset the costs of the IRRs.    
In balancing the need for a cost allocation methodology that assigns costs in a manner 
roughly commensurate with benefits while also being efficient, practically implementable, and 
protecting other transmission customers, the PJM Transmission Owners’ proposed cost allocation 
approach is intended and will achieve the same goals as the cost allocation framework the 
Commission recently approved for Southwest Power Pool, Inc. (“SPP”).159  In particular, as with 
SPP’s cost allocation approach, the PJM Transmission Owners’ cost allocation proposal departs 
from the traditional “but-for” cost assignment model that links specific transmission upgrades with 
specific requests for service.  In the case of SPP, the Commission accepted SPP’s GRID-C 
mechanism for allocating a portion of the costs of transmission facilities to prospective generator 
interconnection customers through fixed per-MW charges that, when collected, are credited back 
against load customer cost responsibility.160 
Although employing different mechanisms due in part to the nature of the Fast Energization 
process proposed by PJM, which is different than the planning process implemented by SPP,161 
the PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal aligns with the same objectives as the SPP cost allocation 
proposal in departing from the traditional “but for” generator interconnection cost allocation 
approach.  Under the proposed Schedule 12 provisions, PJM, as Transmission Provider, will use 
power-flow modeling to calculate the relative contributions of the studied generation and Large 
Load transmission requests to the aggregate transmission system violations on constrained 
facilities addressed by the IRRs.  Those calculations will form the basis of the Interconnection 
Reliability Reinforcement Charge to Zones with Large Load customers, as well as the separate fee 
recovered from generators in each study cycle (the “Generator Reliability Reinforcement Fee”).  
This aggregate approach is tailored to the output of PJM’s Fast Energization Process.  Because 
PJM plans to study multiple generation interconnection and Large Load transmission service 
requests together and identify shared reinforcements addressing multiple system violations, 
individual IRRs will not correspond to individual service requests.162  Such a one-to-one mapping 
would therefore be technically infeasible.  Thus, it is necessary to have a method for allocating the

Page 36 
 
 
 
 
costs of the IRRs that recognizes the integrated and portfolio-based nature of PJM’s proposed Fast 
Energization Process.163 
The resulting allocation maintains a demonstrable relationship between the studied 
requests’ modeled contributions to the identified system needs.  It uses the causal information 
produced through PJM’s study process without attributing individual reinforcements to particular 
requests in recognition of the fact that the study results do not establish that attribution.  This 
approach is consistent with PJM’s existing proportional impact method for allocating costs under 
its New Service Request process, as well as the governing cost allocation standard.  Costs assigned 
to customers must be “roughly commensurate” with the benefits those customers are expected to 
receive, but it need not calculate those benefits with exact precision.164  The PJM Transmission 
Owners’ proposal satisfies that standard by establishing a connection between the studied requests, 
their modeled contributions to the aggregate system violations, and the costs of the shared IRRs 
required to address those violations.  It thereby achieves a roughly commensurate cost allocation 
without attempting to impose a customer-by-customer or project-by-project cost assignment that 
is inconsistent with PJM’s Fast Energization study process.165  That practical approach achieves 
the same objectives as accepted by the Commission in its recent acceptance of SPP’s high impact 
large load (“HILL”) study process and High Impact Large Load Generation Assessment 
(“HILLGA”) framework.166 
The proposed allocation methodology, while complementing PJM’s combined study 
approach, is submitted as an independent FPA section 205 proposal supported on its own merits.  
The just and reasonable basis for the proposed Schedule 12 revisions does not depend on 
Commission acceptance of every feature of PJM’s companion filing.  Rather, the methodology is 
just and reasonable and can operate with any accepted study design that identifies a portfolio of 
transmission solutions and produces the modeled electrical-impact information necessary to 
allocate the resulting costs, regardless of whether that process is confined to Large Load-related 
transmission service requests or also includes generator interconnection requests. 
1. 
PJM Determines Cost Responsibility for the IRR Portfolio Using 
Modeled Electrical Impacts. 
For each Fast Energization Cycle, PJM will identify the IRRs designated for construction 
and determine, based on the provisions set forth in the instant PJM Transmission Owner proposal, 
cost responsibility for the resulting portfolio.  To determine cost responsibility, PJM will use 
power-flow modeling to calculate the proportional impact of each studied service request to the 
aggregate violations on the constrained facilities requiring mitigation through the IRR portfolio.  
This approach begins with the calculation by PJM of distribution factors based on the transfer of 
energy across constrained facilities as identified in the study process.  Proportional impacts for 
 
163  
See Midcontinent Indep. Sys. Operator, Inc. 189 FERC ¶ 61, 108 (2024), order on reh’g, 191 FERC ¶ 61,231 
(2025).

See, e.g., Ill. Com. Comm’n v. FERC, 576 F.3d 470, 476-77 (7th Cir. 2009).

Sw. Power Pool, Inc., 194 FERC ¶ 61,031 (2026) (“SPP HILL Order”).

PDF page 37

Page 37 
 
 
 
 
generators and loads are determined by multiplying the associated distribution factors, if they 
exceed the specified threshold, by the MW capacity for generators or amount of Firm Transmission 
Service requested by an Eligible Customer on behalf of the applicable Large Load.  For MW 
impacts that exceed a defined impact threshold, such impacts will be converted to proportional 
MW impacts representing the pro rata share of all impacts on the same constrained facility.  Then 
the proportional MW impacts are weighted using values as set forth in the PJM Manuals resulting 
in weighted proportional MW impacts.167  As discussed in the Blazunas and Hammer Direct Joint 
Testimony, weighting is important because the same degree of impact is not necessarily 
comparable between different facilities.168  For instance, a 1 MW impact on a 20-mile, 138 kV 
constrained facility is of significantly less consequence than a 1 MW impact on a 150 mile, 500 
kV constrained facility.169  
These features make the proposed tariff revisions a practical measure of cost causation.  
The methodology keys each share to the megawatts the customer actually requested, excludes 
immaterial impacts, and counts only impacts that aggravate a violation.  It therefore satisfies the 
Commission’s long-standing requirement that rates “reflect to some degree the costs actually 
caused by the customer who must pay for them” and that costs be allocated in a manner at least 
roughly commensurate with the benefits the paying entity receives.170  Nor does the methodology 
break new ground.  PJM’s Tariff already provides that all New Service Requests that contribute to 
the need for a Network Upgrade receive cost allocation based on each request’s contribution to the 
reliability violation, in accordance with the PJM Manuals.171  The PJM Transmission Owners’ 
proposal applies that same contribution-based principle, with reasonable modifications to reflect a 
process that studies generator interconnection and Large Load transmission service requests 
together and produces a single portfolio of projects to accommodate those requests.172 
2. 
Aggregate Impacts are Converted into Zonal Cost Responsibility 
Determinations, With Generator Costs Recovered Through a Levelized 
Fee. 
The PJM Transmission Owners’ proposed tariff revisions translate aggregate impact values 
into cost responsibility for the relevant IRRs.  The proportional impacts of Transmission Service 
Requests are combined on a Zonal basis and assigned to the relevant Transmission Owner Zones, 
with the entire initial cost responsibility allocated among to those Zones based on their relative

weighted impacts.173  The costs associated with the IRRs are included in the rate base of the 
Transmission Owners that will construct and own those facilities.  The allocations to other Zones 
are recovered through the Interconnection Reliability Reinforcement Charge, which is discussed 
further below.  The proposed tariff revisions also make these assignments public.  The 
Transmission Provider will complete the cost responsibility analysis before Transmission Service 
Agreements and Generator Interconnection Agreements are executed and before seeking PJM 
Board approval of the reinforcements through their inclusion in the RTEP, and it will post the 
resulting assignments on the PJM website within 30 days of completing the analysis.174  That 
posting requirement responds to, in part, the Commission’s concern that, without sufficient 
transparency, stakeholders may lack visibility into the transmission upgrades, and their associated 
costs, driven by large load additions.175 
The portion of reinforcement costs representing the relative contribution of generators in 
the applicable Fast Energization Process cycle is charged separately to the generators through 
Generator Reliability Reinforcement Fees.176  The Generator Reliability Reinforcement Fee 
translates each generator’s allocated share of reinforcement costs into a levelized payment 
obligation that is collected by PJM and credited to transmission customers in the relevant Zones 
that have been assigned cost responsibility for the IRRs.177  As explained above, each generator’s 
allocated share is determined using a distribution factor-based methodology comparable to PJM’s 
existing proportional impact allocation methodology.  Thus, each generator’s base contribution to 
the overall costs of the applicable portfolio of IRRs is derived using a Commission-endorsed 
approach to determining relative benefits. 
The assessment of a separate fee to generators is just and reasonable.  The Show Cause 
Order was explicit that the Commission did not intend to alter or question existing cost allocation 
methods.178  Consistent with this finding, the PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal preserves the 
foundational approach of including transmission system enhancements in Transmission Owners’ 
rate bases used to determine rates for transmission service, while providing appropriate ratepayer 
protections including the CRA and the additional mechanisms discussed below.  In PJM, load pays 
for transmission service.  However, the PJM Transmission Owners recognize that PJM’s combined 
study approach will consider impacts associated with generator interconnection requests, and thus, 
a contribution from generators to offset the costs of IRRs is appropriate.  Assessing a separate 
charge to generators, in the form of the proposed Generator Reliability Reinforcement Fee, 
provides a means to ensure an appropriate financial contribution from generators, while preserving

the rate base treatment of IRRs consistent with longstanding cost allocation and recovery methods 
in PJM.179  
The PJM Transmission Owners propose to recover Generator Reliability Reinforcement 
Fees in a manner that balances several related goals: (i) maintaining consistency with PJM’s 
proposal to identify IRRs on a portfolio basis, taking into account all applicable generator 
interconnection and Large Load transmission service requests; (ii) providing an ongoing stream of 
credits to transmission customers that extends over a significant portion of the expected life of 
most transmission projects; (iii) recognizing generators’ finite operational lives and particular 
financing needs, while avoiding unduly long-term cost responsibility obligations that could 
discourage needed generation development in the PJM region; and (iv) providing generators with 
upfront cost certainty and commercially reasonable payment terms that avoid creating 
disincentives for generators to locate and build in PJM. 
To balance these considerations, each Generator Interconnection Customer will be 
assessed the applicable Generator Reliability Reinforcement Fee by PJM beginning on the earlier 
of the calendar year in which its facility achieves Commercial Operation or three years after the 
Commercial Operation date specified in its original Generator Interconnection Agreement, and the 
fee will continue for 15 years from that date.180  The Transmission Provider will calculate the fee 
on a levelized basis by applying the generator’s allocated percentage of the total portfolio of IRRs 
to the total estimated gross plant of those IRRs and multiplying the resulting amount by a capital 
recovery factor built from the Annual Carrying Charge Component.181  This Annual Carrying 
Charge Component is the same across all Generator Reliability Reinforcement Fees calculated 
within each cycle.  It is calculated based on the Transmission Owners’ Commission-accepted cost 
of capital: specifically, it is the weighted average of the Weighted Average Cost of Capital, drawn 
from the Attachment H formula rates of each Transmission Owner with reinforcements in the 
applicable Fast Energization Process study cycle that includes the Generator Interconnection 
Customer, weighted by that Transmission Owner’s share of the overall IRR portfolio from the 
applicable study cycle.182 
PJM will invoice one-twelfth of the annual Generator Reliability Reinforcement Fee 
monthly and credit the amounts collected to Firm Transmission Customers.183  Each Zone will 
receive credits in proportion to its share of the annual revenue requirement for the reinforcements 
allocated to Firm Transmission Customers, and each Zone’s credits will be distributed among its 
Firm Transmission Customers based on their pro rata share of the Zone’s annual peak load for the

most recent 12 months ending October 31, appearing as a reduction to the gross monthly rate for 
Firm Transmission Service.184 
This approach achieves a just and reasonable balance among the goals described above. 
First, the 15-year recovery period recognizes the portfolio nature of the IRRs while ensuring that 
credits flow back to transmission customers for a substantial portion of the expected lifespan of a 
transmission portfolio.185  Tying the commencement of the fee to the generator’s Commercial 
Operation Date, with a reasonable allowance for timing changes, also appropriately accommodates 
differences between the timing of generator development and the placement of the associated 
transmission reinforcements in service.  In some instances, a generator may begin paying the fee 
before all transmission facilities in the associated IRR portfolio enter service, providing a relative 
benefit to transmission customers; in others, the generator may begin making payments after 
certain transmission facilities have entered service.  Across the portfolio, however, it is reasonable 
to expect these timing differences to roughly balance out. 
The Commission recently accepted a similarly pragmatic balancing approach in SPP, 
where it approved an expedited process for generators serving HILL that relies on standardized 
financial requirements rather than attempting to calibrate each requirement precisely to the costs 
or risks attributable to each individual generator.186  The Commission found those requirements 
just and reasonable because, among other things, the more stringent financial commitments and 
other eligibility requirements would encourage commercially ready projects.187  The PJM 
Transmission Owners’ approach is, if anything, more tailored: each Generator Reliability 
Reinforcement Fee begins with the generator’s modeled proportional contribution to the violations 
addressed by the applicable IRR portfolio and converts that impact into an administrable, levelized 
payment obligation.  Because the fee will be determined upfront based on the results of the PJM 
system impact study, generators will have certainty as to their costs before making a final 
commitment to move forward and execute an interconnection agreement.  Moreover, by limiting 
the recovery period to 15 years, rather than extending it over the full expected life of the IRRs, the 
proposal avoids imposing commercially unreasonable payment terms that could render otherwise 
viable generation projects unfinanceable.  That combination of cost certainty and reasonable 
payment terms is particularly appropriate given the well-documented challenges associated with 
attracting and installing adequate generating capacity in PJM.188

3. 
Allocations to Transmission Customers Are Recovered Through a 
Dedicated, Formula-Based Charge and Credited to Avoid Double 
Recovery. 
The PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal establishes the Interconnection Reliability 
Reinforcement Charge, which recovers each Transmission Owner’s annual transmission revenue 
requirement for the reinforcements it constructs and owns.189  The Transmission Provider assesses 
the charge to Firm Transmission Customers in each Zone based on the cost responsibility 
assignments determined through the proportional impact methodology described above.190  The 
charge rests on existing, Commission-accepted rates: each Transmission Owner’s revenue 
requirement for a reinforcement is determined under the formula rate in effect for its NITS and 
FCDS.191  The charge is recalculated annually to reflect revised billing determinants and posted 
by October 31 for the following calendar year, and charges to Point-To-Point customers are 
calculated monthly under Tariff, Part II, section 25.192  The Transmission Provider collects the 
charge monthly, in addition to all other transmission charges, and remits the revenues to the 
Transmission Owners that own the relevant IRRs.193  Importantly, application of the 
Interconnection Reliability Reinforcement Charge ensures that IRR costs are allocated only to 
those Zones containing service requests by an Eligible Customer on behalf of the applicable Large 
Load in a given cluster.  Zones with no Large Loads in a cluster will not be allocated any IRR 
costs.  
As discussed in the Blazunas and Hammer Direct Joint Testimony, this dedicated charge 
applies for 15 years after a reinforcement enters service.194  Beginning with the next full calendar 
year after the fifteenth year, the Transmission Provider assigns and recovers the remaining costs 
under sections (a) through (e) of Schedule 12.195  This transition is reasonable.  It confines cost 
responsibility to those Zones containing Large Load and generators whose modeled impacts 
contribute to  the need for reinforcements for a reasonable period that roughly corresponds with 
when it would be expected that some generators would be removed from service or otherwise 
retire, as well as the expectation of significant changes to the topology and use of the transmission 
system.  This 15-year period is also intended to account for what may be a transition or removal 
of certain Large Load facilities and thus their transmission service requests.  Although these 
timelines are, of course, inherently uncertain, the PJM Transmission Owners submit that 15 years 
is a reasonable estimate of when these various factors are likely to result in significant broadening 
of benefits to customers throughout PJM, rather than just the customers in the Zones in which

specific Large Loads are located.196  This mechanism also accounts for the fact that, after over a 
15-year period, broader dynamics of system use and benefit will evolve as economic patterns shift 
and technology changes, resulting in reliance by, and greater benefits to, loads other than the 
original large loads which gave rise to the upgrades.197  These shifts in benefits warrant application 
of the existing Transmission Enhancement Charge cost allocation methodologies, i.e., Solution-
based DFAX and, in the case of Regional Facilities, Load Ratio Share.198 
The proposal also ensures that no customer pays twice for the same facilities.  Because 
each Transmission Owner’s Firm Transmission Service rates under Attachment H already reflect 
its total cost of providing jurisdictional transmission service, including reinforcement costs, 
revenues from the Interconnection Reliability Reinforcement Charge are credited to Firm 
Transmission Customers in the Zone, and to Firm Point-To-Point customers delivering into the 
Zone, in proportion to their Demand Charges and Reserved Capacity, net of any applicable 
incentives.199  This is precisely the crediting mechanism the Show Cause Order contemplated.200  
It also mirrors the Commission’s reasoning in the Co-Located Load Paper Hearing Order, where 
the Commission explained that revenues from NFCDS will be credited against, and thus offset, 
the transmission revenue requirement on which firm transmission rates are based.201 
4. 
Transparency and Direct Wholesale Customer Commitments. 
In addition to the basic cost allocation and rate design features discussed above, the PJM 
Transmission Owners are committing to make, on compliance, necessary changes to their formula 
rate templates to: (1) provide transparency to state regulators and other interested parties regarding 
the costs of transmission facilities being constructed by the Transmission Owner to provide 
transmission service to Large Loads; and (2) adopt a mechanism designed to protect wholesale 
transmission customers not regulated at the retail level (e.g., municipals, cooperatives, and other 
applicable load-serving entities) from having to bear excessive large-load-related transmission 
upgrade costs.202     
With respect to transparency commitments, the Schedule 12 reporting requirements make 
the portfolio allocation results reviewable.  Following completion of the portfolio analysis, PJM 
will post the resulting cost responsibility assignments on its website.  Moreover, each applicable 
Transmission Owner also commits to make, within 120 days of a Commission order accepting this 
filing, a compliance filing to include any necessary additional provisions in its formula rate 
template to identify each transmission project such as IRRs that the Transmission Owner is 
constructing for purposes of providing transmission service to Large Load, along with a calculation

of the annual revenue requirement associated with such projects.203  This information will be 
substantially similar to information that the PJM Transmission Owners already provide in their 
formula rate templates with respect to projects planned through PJM’s RTEP that are allocated 
regionally through Transmission Enhancement Charges (“TEC”).  These additions will serve three 
purposes.  First, they will allow PJM to allocate and invoice Interconnection Reliability 
Reinforcement Charges, which as discussed above will be the mechanism whereby PJM collects 
amounts allocated to Responsible Customers in Zones outside of the Zones in which the IRRs are 
constructed.  Second, in conjunction with the cost responsibility assignments posted by PJM, they 
will satisfy the Commission’s preliminary findings in the Show Cause Order with respect to 
providing transparency regarding the costs associated with transmission upgrades driven by Large 
Load additions.204  Third, by providing this level of cost transparency, state commissions and other 
retail regulators will have access to information to assist them if they choose to allocate some 
amount of transmission rate costs specifically to Large Load customers in their jurisdiction.205  
Additionally, each applicable PJM Transmission Owner commits to include in the above-
referenced compliance filing a surcharge/crediting mechanism designed to protect direct wholesale 
transmission customers in its zone, where applicable, from having to bear disproportionate large-
load-related system upgrade costs.206  These wholesale customers would include municipal 
utilities and electric cooperatives that are not regulated by states and, therefore, cannot be insulated 
by state action against bearing a potentially disproportionate amount of costs associated with 
reinforcements necessary to provide transmission service to Large Load.  The purpose of this 
commitment, and resulting mechanisms, is to ensure that wholesale customers are protected from 
having to bear a share of Large Load-related costs in a situation where there are no Large Loads, 
or relatively fewer Large Loads, in their service areas requesting service in a given PJM study 
cycle.  Where a wholesale customer has Large Load customers that request and are granted 
transmission service through PJM, this mechanism would result in both credits and surcharges, 
since the wholesale customer should bear the cost responsibilities of the upgrades that come out 
of a particular cycle at PJM.   
 The PJM Transmission Owners have deliberately avoided specifying a single mechanism 
for implementing this commitment due to the need for flexibility so that Transmission Owners can 
design and propose wholesale customer protection mechanisms that are consistent with their own 
individual circumstances, including any applicable retail allocation or charge mechanisms.207  One 
potential approach involves a surcharge/credit approach pursuant to which a Transmission Owner 
would calculate, in addition to its total NITS revenue requirement, a revenue requirement specific

constructing, which will assist in facilitating retail regulators within multi-Transmission Owner Zones to allocate costs 
relevant to retail customers within their states.

Page 44 
 
 
 
 
to large load-driven upgrades.  Using that information, a credit would be calculated and provided 
to wholesale transmission customers to the extent they do not have Large Load customers, or 
relatively fewer Large Load customers, driving the upgrades in a specific cluster, based on their 
pro rata share of overall NITS load.  The credit would be funded by the large loads (specifically, 
the Electric Distribution Company/Load Serving Entity representing those Large Loads) with 
relatively greater Large Load customers in a cluster through a surcharge to those entities.  
Regardless, the PJM Transmission Owners commit to include their proposed direct wholesale 
customer protection mechanisms in the compliance filings. 
5. 
Schedule 12, Section (f) Produces Just and Reasonable Rates and is 
not Unduly Discriminatory or Preferential. 
The proposed tariff revisions in Schedule 12, Section (f) satisfy the just and reasonable 
standard of FPA section 205 because they align cost responsibility with cost causation and benefits 
at every step.  The Commission identified the central risk in this proceeding as the shifting of large 
load-driven transmission costs onto “other transmission customers that neither caused the need for 
nor benefit commensurately from those Network Upgrades.”208  The Commission further observed 
that, when Network Upgrade costs are rolled into a Transmission Owner’s revenue requirement, 
transmission customers other than the Eligible Customer may bear a portion of those costs.209  As 
discussed above, the PJM Transmission Owners’ proposal addresses these concerns through a 
combination of the proportional impact methodology, 15-year cost allocation transition 
mechanism, and the assessment and crediting of the Interconnection Reliability Reinforcement and 
Generator Reliability Recovery Fees.  These provisions will work in tandem with the proposed 
CRA or similar agreements to protect against the risk of shifting costs to transmission customers 
for facilities from which they receive no benefit.210 
The resulting rates are also cost-based and verifiable.  Every input to the charge and the 
fee, from the revenue requirement to the cost of capital, comes from Transmission Owner formula 
rates already on file with the Commission, and annual recalculation and public posting keep the 
charges current and transparent.211  The Commission therefore has a clear basis on which to find 
that proposed Schedule 12, Section (f) yields just and reasonable rates. 
Section (f) appropriately distinguishes between generation and Large Load-related 
transmission service requests while providing a consistent basis for measuring their respective 
contributions to the need for IRRs.  Requests included in the same Cycle are evaluated using the 
same distribution factor-based methodology, thresholds, and weighting rules, ensuring that their 
relative electrical impacts are measured on a consistent basis.212  That common measurement does 
not, however, equate generation and Large Load customers for rate design purposes.  Rather, the

Show Cause Order at P 81.

Proposed Schedule 12, Sections (f)(1), (f)(2)(C), (f)(3), (f)(5).

proposed tariff provisions assign and recover the resulting costs consistent with longstanding rate 
structures while accounting for the portfolio nature of IRRs that will be identified in PJM’s Fast 
Energization Process: IRRs will be rolled into Zonal transmission rates and recovered from 
transmission customers through the Interconnection Reliability Reinforcement Charge, while 
Generator Interconnection Customers bear a fair share of IRR costs through the Generator 
Reliability Reinforcement Fee.213  The proposal thus distinguishes between generation and load 
where their differing circumstances warrant different rate treatment, while applying a consistent 
cost causation methodology to determine their respective contributions to the IRRs. 
Section (f) also resolves a potential source of discrimination the Commission identified in 
the Co-Located Load Paper Hearing Order.  There, the Commission found that the lack of clear 
tariff provisions led to disparate treatment because Transmission Owners took different approaches 
and created the risk that costs would not be allocated consistent with cost causation principles.214  
Section (f) adopts a uniform rule applied across the PJM region.  At the same time, Section (f) 
confines itself to Commission-jurisdictional transmission rates, which the Commission has found 
are directly affected by the costs of upgrades needed to serve Large Loads,215 and it leaves 
undisturbed state ratepayer protections such as state-jurisdictional Large Load tariffs.216 
For these reasons, the Commission should find that proposed revisions to Section (f) of 
Schedule 12 are just and reasonable and not unduly discriminatory or preferential and they satisfy 
the Show Cause Order’s directives regarding cost transparency, mitigation of cost shifting among 
transmission customers, and the crediting of Large Load payments toward transmission revenue 
requirements. 
IV. 
EFFECTIVE DATE AND IMPLEMENTATION 
The PJM Transmission Owners understand that PJM intends to request an effective date of 
March 16, 2027 for the proposed revisions to Parts I, III, VI, VII, IX, XI, Attachment F-3, 
Attachment F-3A, Attachment F-4, and certain parts of Part VIII to the Tariff.  For the remaining 
Part VIII revisions associated with the Fast Energization Process, PJM intends to request an 
indeterminate effective date of 12/31/9998, rather than the effective date requested in its August 
17 Compliance Filing (for which PJM requested a March 16, 2027 effective date).  The PJM 
Transmission Owners further understand that PJM intends to request a 60-day comment period for 
its companion filing.   
Accordingly, given the interrelated nature of the filings, the PJM Transmission Owners 
request an effective date of March 16, 2027 for their proposed revisions to Tariff, Part XI and 
Schedules 20, 21, and 22, and an effective date of 12/31/9998 for the proposed pro forma CRA 
(Part IX, Subpart P); Tariff, Part VIII, Subpart N, section 441; and Schedule 12 revisions proposed

Definitions – R - S
Ramping Capability:
“Ramping Capability” shall mean the sustained rate of change of generator output, in megawatts 
per minute.
Real-time Congestion Price:
“Real-time Congestion Price” shall mean the Congestion Price resulting from the Office of the 
Interconnection’s dispatch of the PJM Interchange Energy Market in the Operating Day.
Real-time Loss Price:
“Real-time Loss Price” shall mean the Loss Price resulting from the Office of the 
Interconnection’s dispatch of the PJM Interchange Energy Market in the Operating Day.
Real-time Energy Market:
“Real-time Energy Market” shall mean the purchase or sale of energy and payment of 
Transmission Congestion Charges for quantity deviations from the Day-ahead Energy Market in 
the Operating Day.
Real-time Offer:
“Real-time Offer” shall mean a new offer or an update to a Market Seller’s existing cost-based or 
market-based offer for a clock hour, submitted for use after the close of the Day-ahead Energy 
Market.
Real-time Prices:
“Real-time Prices” shall mean the Locational Marginal Prices resulting from the Office of the 
Interconnection’s dispatch of the PJM Interchange Energy Market in the Operating Day.
Real-time Settlement Interval:
“Real-time Settlement Interval” shall mean the interval used by settlements, which shall be every 
five minutes.
Real-time System Energy Price:
“Real-time System Energy Price” shall mean the System Energy Price resulting from the Office 
of the Interconnection’s dispatch of the PJM Interchange Energy Market in the Operating Day.
Reasonable Efforts:
Page 2

PDF page 51

“Reasonable Efforts” shall mean, with respect to any action required to be made, attempted, or 
taken by an Interconnection Party or by a Construction Party under Tariff, Part IV or Part VI, an 
Interconnection Service Agreement, or a Construction Service Agreement, such efforts as are 
timely and consistent with Good Utility Practice and with efforts that such party would undertake 
for the protection of its own interests.
Receiving Party:
“Receiving Party” shall mean the entity receiving the capacity and energy transmitted by the 
Transmission Provider to Point(s) of Delivery.
Referral:
“Referral” shall mean a formal report of the Market Monitoring Unit to the Commission for 
investigation of behavior of a Market Participant, of behavior of PJM, or of a market design 
flaw, pursuant to Tariff, Attachment M, section IV.I.
Reference Resource:
“Reference Resource” shall mean a combustion turbine generating station, configured with a 
single General Electric Frame 7HA turbine with evaporative cooling, Selective Catalytic 
Reduction technology all CONE Areas, dual fuel capability, and a heat rate of 9.134 Mmbtu/ 
MWh. 
Regional Entity:
“Regional Entity” shall have the same meaning specified in the Operating Agreement.
Regional Transmission Expansion Plan:
“Regional Transmission Expansion Plan” shall mean the plan prepared by the Office of the 
Interconnection pursuant to Operating Agreement, Schedule 6 for the enhancement and 
expansion of the Transmission System in order to meet the demands for firm transmission 
service in the PJM Region.
Regional Transmission Group (RTG):
“Regional Transmission Group” or “RTG” shall mean a voluntary organization of transmission 
owners, transmission users and other entities approved by the Commission to efficiently 
coordinate transmission planning (and expansion), operation and use on a regional (and 
interregional) basis.
Regulation:
“Regulation” shall mean the capability of a specific generation resource or Demand Resource 
with appropriate telecommunications, control and response capability to separately increase and 
Page 3

PDF page 52

decrease its output or adjust load in response to a regulating control signal, in accordance with 
the specifications in the PJM Manuals.  Regulation is inclusive of Regulation-Down Service and 
Regulation-Up Service.
Regulation-Down Requirement:
“Regulation-Down Requirement” shall mean the required megawatts of performance-adjusted 
Regulation-Down Service capability to be maintained in a Regulation Zone. The Regulation-
Down Requirement is defined as a set megawatt value by hour in accordance with the PJM 
Manuals, and can increase to account for additional operational uncertainty.
Regulation-Down Service
“Regulation-Down Service” shall mean the capability of a specific generation resource or 
Demand Resource with appropriate telecommunications and response capability to increase and 
decrease its output in the downward range from a set point or adjust load in response to a 
regulating-down control signal, in accordance with the specification in the PJM Manuals.
Regulation-Up Requirement:
“Regulation-Up Requirement” shall mean the required megawatts of performance-adjusted
Regulation-Up Service capability to be maintained in a Regulation Zone. The Regulation-Up
Requirement is defined as a set megawatt value by hour in accordance with the PJM Manuals,
and can increase to account for additional operational uncertainty.
Regulation-Up Service
“Regulation-Up Service” shall mean the capability of a specific generation resource or Demand
Resource with appropriate telecommunications and response capability to increase and decrease
its output in the upward range from a set point or adjust load in response to a regulating-up
control signal, in accordance with the specification in the PJM Manuals.
Regulation Zone:
“Regulation Zone” shall mean any of those one or more geographic areas, each consisting of a 
combination of one or more Control Zone(s) as designated by the Office of the Interconnection 
in the PJM Manuals, relevant to provision of, and requirements for, regulation service.
Relevant Electric Retail Regulatory Authority:
“Relevant Electric Retail Regulatory Authority” shall mean an entity that has jurisdiction over 
and establishes prices and policies for competition for providers of retail electric service to end-
customers, such as the city council for a municipal utility, the governing board of a cooperative 
utility, the state public utility commission or any other such entity.
Reliability Assurance Agreement or PJM Reliability Assurance Agreement:

PDF page 53

“Reliability Assurance Agreement” or “PJM Reliability Assurance Agreement” shall mean that 
certain Reliability Assurance Agreement Among Load Serving Entities in the PJM Region, on 
file with FERC as PJM Interconnection L.L.C. Rate Schedule FERC No. 44, and as amended 
from time to time thereafter.
Reliability Pricing Model Auction:
“Reliability Pricing Model Auction” or “RPM Auction” shall mean the Base Residual Auction or 
any Incremental Auction, or, for the 2016/2017 and 2017/2018 Delivery Years, any Capacity 
Performance Transition Incremental Auction.
Required Transmission Enhancements:
“Regional Transmission Enhancements” shall mean enhancements and expansions of the 
Transmission System that (1) a Regional Transmission Expansion Plan developed pursuant to 
Operating Agreement, Schedule 6 or (2) any joint planning or coordination agreement between 
PJM and another region or transmission planning authority set forth in Tariff, Schedule 12-
Appendix B (“Appendix B Agreement”) designates one or more of the Transmission Owner(s) to 
construct and own or finance.  Required Transmission Enhancements shall also include 
enhancements and expansions of facilities in another region or planning authority that meet the 
definition of transmission facilities pursuant to FERC’s Uniform System of Accounts or have 
been classified as transmission facilities in a ruling by FERC addressing such facilities 
constructed pursuant to an Appendix B Agreement cost responsibility for which has been 
assigned at least in part to PJM pursuant to such Appendix B Agreement. 
Reserved Capacity:
“Reserved Capacity” shall mean the maximum amount of capacity and energy that the 
Transmission Provider agrees to transmit for the Transmission Customer over the Transmission 
Provider’s Transmission System between the Point(s) of Receipt and the Point(s) of Delivery 
under Tariff, Part II.  Reserved Capacity shall be expressed in terms of whole megawatts on a 
sixty (60) minute interval (commencing on the clock hour) basis.
Reserve Penalty Factor:
“Reserve Penalty Factor” shall mean the cost, in $/MWh, associated with being unable to meet a 
specific reserve requirement in a Reserve Zone or Reserve Sub-zone.  A Reserve Penalty Factor 
will be defined for each reserve requirement in a Reserve Zone or Reserve Sub-zone.
Reserve Sub-zone:
“Reserve Sub-zone” shall mean any of those geographic areas wholly contained within a Reserve 
Zone, consisting of a combination of a portion of one or more Control Zone(s) as designated by 
the Office of the Interconnection in the PJM Manuals, relevant to provision of, and requirements 
for, reserve service.

Reserve Zone:
“Reserve Zone” shall mean any of those geographic areas consisting of a combination of one or 
more Control Zone(s), as designated by the Office of the Interconnection in the PJM Manuals, 
relevant to provision of, and requirements for, reserve service.
Residual Auction Revenue Rights:
“Residual Auction Revenue Rights” shall mean incremental stage 1 Auction Revenue Rights 
created within a Planning Period by an increase in transmission system capability, including the 
return to service of existing transmission capability, that was not modeled pursuant to Operating 
Agreement, Schedule 1, section 7.5 and the parallel provisions of  Tariff, Attachment K-

and the parallel provisions of Tariff, Attachment K-Appendix, section 7.4.2(h), and, if modeled, 
would have increased the amount of stage 1 Auction Revenue Rights allocated pursuant to 
Operating Agreement, Schedule 1, section 7.4.2 and the parallel provisions of Tariff, Attachment 
K-Appendix, section 7.4.2; provided that, the foregoing notwithstanding, Residual Auction 
Revenue Rights shall exclude: 1) Incremental Auction Revenue Rights allocated pursuant to 
Tariff, Part VI;  and 2) Auction Revenue Rights allocated to entities that are assigned cost 
responsibility pursuant to Operating Agreement, Schedule 6 for  transmission upgrades that 
create such rights.
Residual Metered Load:
“Residual Metered Load” shall mean all load remaining in an electric distribution company’s 
fully metered franchise area(s) or service territory(ies) after all nodally priced load of entities 
serving load in such area(s) or territory(ies) has been carved out.
Resource Substitution Charge:
“Resource Substitution Charge” shall mean a charge assessed on Capacity Market Buyers in an 
Incremental Auction to recover the cost of replacement Capacity Resources.
Responsible Customer:

Transmission Service, Point-to-Point Transmission Service and/or Network Integration

Schedule 12.

Secondary Reserve:

clearing price or submitted as a Sell Offer with a price bid. A Load Serving Entity's Sell Offer

Self-Supply Entity:

100kW.

“Small Generation Resource” shall mean an Interconnection Customer’s device of 20 MW or 
less for the production and/or storage for later injection of electricity identified in an

Facilities.  This term shall include Energy Storage Resources and/or other devices for storage for 
later injection of energy.
Small Inverter Facility:
“Small Inverter Facility” shall mean an Energy Resource that is a certified small inverter-based

Small Inverter ISA:
“Small Inverter ISA” shall mean an agreement among Transmission Provider, Interconnection

Facility under Tariff, Part IV, section 112B.
Special Member:
“Special Member” shall mean an entity that satisfies the requirements of Operating Agreement, 
Schedule 1, section 1.5A.02, and the parallel provisions of Tariff, Attachment K-Appendix, 
section 1.5A.02, or the special membership provisions established under the Emergency Load 
Response and Pre-Emergency Load Response Programs.
Spot Market Backup:
“Spot Market Backup” shall mean the purchase of energy from, or the delivery of energy to, the 
PJM Interchange Energy Market in quantities sufficient to complete the delivery or receipt 
obligations of a bilateral contract that has been curtailed or interrupted for any reason.
Spot Market Energy:
“Spot Market Energy” shall mean energy bought or sold by Market Participants through the PJM 
Interchange Energy Market at System Energy Prices determined as specified in Operating 
Agreement, Schedule 1, section 2, and the parallel provisions of Tariff, Attachment K-Appendix, 
section 2.
Start Additional Labor Costs:
“Start Additional Labor Costs” shall mean additional labor costs for startup required above 
normal station manning levels. 
Start Fuel:
Page 11

PDF page 60

For units without a soak process, “Start Fuel” shall consist of fuel consumed from first fire of the 
start process to first breaker closing, plus any fuel expended from last breaker opening to 
shutdown.
For units with a soak process, “Start Fuel” is fuel consumed from first fire of the start process 
(initial reactor criticality for nuclear units) to dispatchable output (including auxiliary boiler 
fuel), plus any fuel expended from last breaker opening to shutdown, excluding normal plant 
heating/auxiliary equipment fuel requirements.  Start Fuel included for each temperature state 
from breaker closure to dispatchable output shall not exceed the unit specific soak time period 
reviewed and approved as part of the unit-specific parameter process detailed in Tariff, 
Attachment K-Appendix, section 6.6(c) or the defaults below:
•
Cold Soak Time = 0.73 * unit specific Minimum Run Time (in hours)
•
Intermediate Soak Time = 0.61 * unit specific Minimum Run Time (in hours)
•
Hot Soak Time = 0.43 * unit specific Minimum Run Time (in hours)  
Start-Up Costs:
“Start-Up Costs” shall consist primarily of the cost of fuel, as determined by the unit’s start heat 
input (adjusted by the performance factor) times the fuel cost.  It also includes operating costs, 
Maintenance Adders, emissions allowances/adders, and station service cost.  Start-Up Costs can 
vary with the unit offline time being categorized in three unit temperature conditions: hot, 
intermediate and cold.
For units with a steam turbine and a soak process (nuclear, steam, and combined cycle), “Start 
Fuel” is fuel consumed from first fire of start process (initial reactor criticality for nuclear units): 
Start-Up Costs shall mean the net unit costs from PJM’s notification to the level at which the unit 
can follow PJM’s dispatch, and from last breaker open to shutdown.
For units without a steam turbine and no soak process (engines, combustion turbines, 
Intermittent Resources, and Energy Storage Resources): Start-Up Costs shall mean the unit costs 
from PJM’s notification to first breaker close and from last breaker open to shutdown.  
State:
“State” shall mean the District of Columbia and any State or Commonwealth of the United 
States.
State Commission:
“State Commission” shall mean any state regulatory agency having jurisdiction over retail 
electricity sales in any State in the PJM Region.
State Estimator:
Page 12

PDF page 61

“State Estimator” shall mean the computer model of power flows specified in Operating 
Agreement, Schedule 1, section 2.3 and the parallel provisions of Tariff, Attachment K-
Appendix, section 2.3.
State Subsidy:
“State Subsidy” shall mean a direct or indirect payment, concession, rebate, subsidy, non-
bypassable consumer charge, or other financial benefit that is as a result of any action, mandated 
process, or sponsored process of a state government, a political subdivision or agency of a state, 
or an electric cooperative formed pursuant to state law, and that 
(1) is derived from or connected to the procurement of (a) electricity or electric 
generation capacity sold at wholesale in interstate commerce, or (b) an attribute of the 
generation process for electricity or electric generation capacity sold at wholesale in 
interstate commerce; or
(2) will support the construction, development, or operation of a new or existing Capacity 
Resource; or 
(3) could have the effect of allowing the unit to clear in any PJM capacity auction. 
Notwithstanding the foregoing, State Subsidy shall not include (a) payments, concessions, 
rebates, subsidies, or incentives designed to incent, or participation in a program, contract or 
other arrangement that utilizes criteria designed to incent or promote, general industrial 
development in an area or designed to incent siting facilities in that county or locality rather than 
another county or locality; (b) state action that imposes a tax or assesses a charge utilizing the 
parameters of a regional program on a given set of resources notwithstanding the tax or cost 
having indirect benefits on resources not subject to the tax or cost (e.g., Regional Greenhouse 
Gas Initiative); (c) any indirect benefits to a Capacity Resource as a result of any transmission 
project approved as part of the Regional Transmission Expansion Plan; (d) any contract, legally 
enforceable obligation, or rate pursuant to the Public Utility Regulatory Policies Act or any other 
state-administered federal regulatory program (e.g., the Cross-State Air Pollution Rule); (e) any 
revenues from the sale or allocation, either direct or indirect, to an Entity Providing Supply 
Services to Default Retail Service Provider where such entity’s obligations was awarded through 
a state default procurement auction that was subject to independent oversight by a consultant or 
manager who certifies that the auction was conducted through a non-discriminatory and 
competitive bidding process, subject to the below condition, and provided further that nothing 
herein would exempt a Capacity Resource that would otherwise be subject to the minimum offer 
price rule pursuant to this Tariff; (f) any revenues for providing capacity as part of an FRR 
Capacity Plan or through bilateral transactions with FRR Entities; or (g) any voluntary and arm’s 
length bilateral transaction (including but not limited to those reported pursuant to Tariff, 
Attachment DD, section 4.6), such as a power purchase agreement or other similar contract 
where the buyer is a Self-Supply Entity and the transaction is (1) a short term transaction (one-
year or less) or (2) a long-term transaction that is the result of a competitive process that was not 
fuel-specific and is not used for the purpose of supporting uneconomic construction, 
development, or operation of the subject Capacity Resource, provided however that if the Self-
Supply Entity is responsible for offering the Capacity Resource into an RPM Auction, the 
specified amount of installed capacity purchased by such Self-Supply Entity shall be considered 
to receive a State Subsidy in the same manner, under the same conditions, and to the same extent 
as any other Capacity Resource of a Self-Supply Entity.  For purposes of subsection (e) of this 
Page 13

PDF page 62

definition, a state default procurement auction that has been certified to be a result of a non-
discriminatory and competitive bidding process shall:
(i)
have no conditions based on the ownership (except supplier diversity 
requirements or limits), location (except to meet PJM deliverability 
requirements), affiliation, fuel type, technology, or emissions of any resources or 
supply (except state-mandated renewable portfolio standards for which Capacity 
Resources are separately subject to the minimum offer price rule or eligible for an 
exemption);
(ii)
result in contracts between an Entity Providing Supply Services to Default Retail 
Service Provider and the electric distribution company for a retail default 
generation supply product and none of those contracts require that the retail 
obligation be sourced from any specific Capacity Resource or resource type as set 
forth in subsection (i) above; and
(iii)
establish market-based compensation for a retail default generation supply 
product that retail customers can avoid paying for by obtaining supply from a 
competitive retail supplier of their choice.
State of Charge:
“State of Charge” shall mean the quantity of physical energy stored in an Energy Storage 
Resource Model Participant or in the storage component of a Hybrid Resource in proportion to 
its maximum State of Charge capability. State of Charge is quantified as defined in the PJM 
Manuals.
State of Charge Management:
“State of Charge Management” shall mean the control of State of Charge of an Energy Storage 
Resource Market Participant or Hybrid Resource using minimum and maximum discharge (and, 
as applicable, charge) limits, changes in operating mode (as applicable), discharging (and, as 
applicable, charging) offer curves, and self-scheduling of non-dispatchable sales (and, as 
applicable, purchases) of energy in the PJM markets.  State of Charge Management shall not 
interfere with the obligation of a Market Seller of an Energy Storage Resource Model Participant
or of a Hybrid Resource to follow PJM dispatch, consistent with all other resources.
Station Power:
“Station Power” shall mean energy used for operating the electric equipment on the site of a 
generation facility located in the PJM Region or for the heating, lighting, air-conditioning and 
office equipment needs of buildings on the site of such a generation facility that are used in the 
operation, maintenance, or repair of the facility.  Station Power does not include any energy (i) 
used to power synchronous condensers; (ii) used for pumping at a pumped storage facility; (iii) 
used in association with restoration or black start service; or (iv) that is Direct Charging Energy.
Sub-Annual Resource Constraint:
Page 14

PDF page 63

“Sub-Annual Resource Constraint” shall mean, for the 2017/2018 Delivery Year and for FRR 
Capacity Plans the 2017/2018 and 2018/2019 Delivery Years, for the PJM Region or for each 
LDA for which the Office of the Interconnection is required under Tariff, Attachment DD, 
section 5.10(a) to establish a separate VRR Curve for a Delivery Year, a limit on the total 
amount of Unforced Capacity that can be committed as Limited Demand Resources and 
Extended Summer Demand Resources for the 2017/2018 Delivery Year in the PJM Region or in 
such LDA, calculated as the Sub-Annual Resource Reliability Target for the PJM Region or for 
such LDA, respectively, minus the Short-Term Resource Procurement Target for the PJM 
Region or for such LDA, respectively. 
Sub-Annual Resource Price Decrement:
“Sub-Annual Resource Price Decrement” shall mean, for the 2017/2018 Delivery Year, a 
difference between the clearing price for Extended Summer Demand Resources and the clearing 
price for Annual Resources, representing the cost to procure additional Annual Resources out of 
merit order when the Sub-Annual Resource Constraint is binding. 
Sub-Annual Resource Reliability Target: 
“Sub-Annual Reliability Target” for the PJM Region or an LDA, shall mean the maximum 
amount of the combination of Extended Summer Demand Resources and Limited Demand 
Resources in Unforced Capacity determined by PJM to be consistent with the maintenance of 
reliability, stated in Unforced Capacity, that shall be used to calculate the Minimum Annual 
Resource Requirement for Delivery Years through May 31, 2017 and the Sub-Annual Resource 
Constraint for the 2017/2018 and 2018/2019 Delivery Years.  As more fully set forth in the PJM 
Manuals, PJM calculates the Sub-Annual Resource Reliability Target, by first determining a 
reference annual loss of load expectation (“LOLE”) assuming no Demand Resources.  The 
calculation for the unconstrained portion of the PJM Region uses a daily distribution of loads 
under a range of weather scenarios (based on the most recent load forecast and iteratively 
shifting the load distributions to result in the Installed Reserve Margin established for the 
Delivery Year in question) and a weekly capacity distribution (based on the cumulative capacity 
availability distributions developed for the Installed Reserve Margin study for the Delivery Year 
in question).  The calculation for each relevant LDA uses a daily distribution of loads under a 
range of weather scenarios (based on the most recent load forecast for the Delivery Year in 
question) and a weekly capacity distribution (based on the cumulative capacity availability 
distributions developed for the Capacity Emergency Transfer Objective study for the Delivery 
Year in question).  For the relevant LDA calculation, the weekly capacity distributions are 
adjusted to reflect the Capacity Emergency Transfer Limit for the Delivery Year in question.
For both the PJM Region and LDA analyses, PJM then models the commitment of varying 
amounts of DR (displacing otherwise committed generation) as interruptible from May 1 through 
October 31 and unavailable from November 1 through April 30 and calculates the LOLE at each 
DR level.  The Extended Summer DR Reliability Target is the DR amount, stated as a percentage 
of the unrestricted peak load, that produces no more than a ten percent increase in the LOLE, 
compared to the reference value.  The Sub-Annual Resource Reliability Target shall be 
expressed as a percentage of the forecasted peak load of the PJM Region or such LDA and is 
Page 15

PDF page 64

converted to Unforced Capacity by multiplying [the reliability target percentage] times [the 
Forecast Pool Requirement] times [the DR Factor] times [the forecasted peak load of the PJM 
Region or such LDA, reduced by the amount of load served under the FRR Alternative].
Sub-meter:
“Sub-meter” shall mean a metering point for electricity consumption that does not include all 
electricity consumption for the end-use customer as defined by the electric distribution company 
account number.  PJM shall only accept sub-meter load data from end-use customers for 
measurement and verification of Regulation service as set forth in the Economic Load Response 
rules and PJM Manuals.
Summer-Period Capacity Performance Resource:
“Summer-Period Capacity Performance Resource” shall have the same meaning specified in 
Tariff, Attachment DD, section 5.5A.
Surplus Interconnection Customer:
“Surplus Interconnection Customer” shall mean either an Interconnection Customer whose 
Generating Facility is already interconnected to the PJM Transmission System or one of its 
affiliates, or an unaffiliated entity that submits a Surplus Interconnection Request to utilize 
Surplus Interconnection Service within the Transmission System in the PJM Region.  A Surplus 
Interconnection Customer is not a New Service Customer.   
Surplus Interconnection Request:
“Surplus Interconnection Request” shall mean a request submitted by a Surplus Interconnection 
Customer, pursuant to Tariff, Attachment RR, to utilize Surplus Interconnection Service within 
the Transmission System in the PJM Region.  A Surplus Interconnection Request is not a New 
Service Request.
Surplus Interconnection Service:
“Surplus Interconnection Service” shall mean any unneeded portion of Interconnection Service 
established in an Interconnection Service Agreement, such that if Surplus Interconnection 
Service is utilized, the total amount of Interconnection Service at the Point of Interconnection 
would remain the same.
Switching and Tagging Rules:
“Switching and Tagging Rules” shall mean the switching and tagging procedures of 
Interconnected Transmission Owners and Interconnection Customer as they may be amended 
from time to time. 
Synchronized Reserve:
Page 16

PDF page 65

“Synchronized Reserve” shall mean the reserve capability of generation resources that can be 
converted fully into energy or Economic Load Response Participant resources whose demand 
can be reduced within ten minutes from the request of the Office of the Interconnection 
dispatcher, and is provided by equipment that is electrically synchronized to the Transmission 
System.
Synchronized Reserve Event:
“Synchronized Reserve Event” shall mean a request from the Office of the Interconnection to 
generation resources and/or Economic Load Response Participant resources able, assigned or 
self-scheduled to provide Synchronized Reserve in one or more specified Reserve Zones or 
Reserve Sub-zones, within ten minutes, to increase the energy output or reduce load by the 
amount of assigned or self-scheduled Synchronized Reserve capability.
Synchronized Reserve Requirement:
“Synchronized Reserve Requirement” shall mean the megawatts required to be maintained in a 
Reserve Zone or Reserve Sub-zone as Synchronized Reserve, absent any increase to account for 
additional reserves scheduled to address operational uncertainty.  The Synchronized Reserve 
Requirement is calculated in accordance with the PJM Manuals.  This requirement can only be 
satisfied by Synchronized Reserve resources.
System Condition:
“System Condition” shall mean a specified condition on the Transmission Provider’s system or 
on a neighboring system, such as a constrained transmission element or flowgate, that may 
trigger Curtailment of Long-Term Firm Point-to-Point Transmission Service using the 
curtailment priority pursuant to Tariff, Part II, section 13.6.  Such conditions must be identified 
in the Transmission Customer’s Service Agreement.
System Energy Price:
“System Energy Price” shall mean the energy component of the Locational Marginal Price, 
which is the price at which the Market Seller has offered to supply an additional increment of 
energy from a resource, calculated as specified in Operating Agreement, Schedule 1, section 2 
and the parallel provisions of Tariff, Attachment K-Appendix, section 2.
System Impact Study:
“System Impact Study” shall mean an assessment by the Transmission Provider of (i) the 
adequacy of the Transmission System to accommodate a Completed Application, an 
Interconnection Request or an Upgrade Request, (ii) whether any additional costs may be 
incurred in order to provide such transmission service or to accommodate an Interconnection 
Request, and (iii) with respect to an Interconnection Request, an estimated date that an 
Interconnection Customer’s Customer Facility can be interconnected with the Transmission 
Page 17

PDF page 66

System and an estimate of the Interconnection Customer’s cost responsibility for the 
interconnection; and (iv) with respect to an Upgrade Request, the estimated cost of the requested 
system upgrades or expansion, or of the cost of the system upgrades or expansion, necessary to 
provide the requested incremental rights.
System Protection Facilities:
“System Protection Facilities” shall refer to the equipment required to protect (i) the 
Transmission System, other delivery systems and/or other generating systems connected to the 
Transmission System from faults or other electrical disturbance occurring at or on the Customer 
Facility, and (ii) the Customer Facility from faults or other electrical system disturbance 
occurring on the Transmission System or on other delivery systems and/or other generating 
systems to which the Transmission System is directly or indirectly connected.  System Protection 
Facilities shall include such protective and regulating devices as are identified in the Applicable 
Technical Requirements and Standards or that are required by Applicable Laws and Regulations 
or other Applicable Standards, or as are otherwise necessary to protect personnel and equipment 
and to minimize deleterious effects to the Transmission System arising from the Customer 
Facility.

Facilities Study:
“Facilities Study” shall be an engineering study conducted by the Transmission Provider (in 
coordination with the affected Transmission Owner(s)) to: (1) determine the required 
modifications to the Transmission Provider’s Transmission System necessary to implement the 
conclusions of the System Impact Studies; and (2) complete any additional studies or analyses 
documented in the System Impact Studies or required by PJM Manuals, and determine the 
required modifications to the Transmission Provider's Transmission System based on the 
conclusions of such additional studies.
